Quantization and Algebraic Index
Si Li

Abstract
This article reviews the program on connecting Batalin-Vilkovisky (BV) quantization with index theories of algebraic type. We explain how the classical algebraic index theorem can be proved in terms of BV quantization of topological quantum mechanics. This is generalized to 2d chiral CFT in which we present an elliptic chiral analog of the algebraic index theory. As an application, we show how the generating function of all genus Gromov-Witten invariants on elliptic curves is mirror equivalent to an elliptic chiral index in the mirror BCOV theory.



Dedicated to Prof. S.-T. Yau on the occasion of his 75th Birthday

Contents

1
Introduction

Quantum Field Theory
Homological Algebra
Index Theory

It is well known that the Atiyah-Singer index theorem is closely related to supersymmetric/topological quantum mechanics
[
2
,
26
,
49
,
50
]
. Though not rigorous, this physics interpretation provides a clear and deep insight into the origin of index theorem via the geometry of the loop space. As a natural generalization, one can replace the loop by the two-dimensional torus. This leads to Witten’s proposal
[
51
,
52
]
for the index of Dirac operators on loop spaces.

In
[
22
,
42
]
, Fedosov and Nest-Tsygan established the algebraic index theorem for deformation quantized algebras as the algebraic analogue of Atiyah-Singer index theorem. It was further shown
[
43
]
that the original Atiyah-Singer index theorem can be deduced from this algebraic one. In
[
29
,
31
]
, we established an exact connection between the algebraic index theorem and topological quantum mechanics via a trace map constructed in the Batalin-Vilkovisky(BV) formalism
[
4
]
. This sets up a mathematical understanding of the physics approach to index theorem in terms of an exact low-energy effective quantum field theory
[
31
]
. Such connection between quantization and algberaic index can be naturally extended to quantum field theory on other geometric objects, such as the torus. In
[
37
,
30
]
, we developed the effective BV quantization theory of two-dimensional chiral theory and established a chiral analogue of the algebraic index theory on the torus.

This paper reviews the program on BV quantization and index theories of algebraic type developed in
[
29
,
31
,
37
,
30
]
. Here we summarize the main structures.

Let us denote by
\mathbf{k}
the field of Laurent series
\mathbb{C}((\hbar))
. Roughly speaking, BV quantization in quantum field theory on
X
leads to the following data (we will give more details in the body of the text)

1.

A factorization algebra of local observables (we follow the set-up in
[
16
]
).

\mathrm{Obs}:\quad\text{a $\mathbf{k}$-module equipped with certain algebra structure.}

It carries an algebraic structure called factorization product (or operator product expansion in physics terminology).

2.

A (factorization) chain complex

C_{\bullet}(\mathrm{Obs}):\quad\text{a $\mathbf{k}$-chain complex},\qquad d:\quad\text{the differential}.

It captures the algebraic structure and global information from local observables.

3.

A BV algebra
(O_{\mathrm{BV}},\Delta)

O_{\mathrm{BV}}:\quad\text{a BV algebra over}\ \mathbb{C},\qquad\text{$\Delta:\quad$ the BV operator}

together with a BV integration map

\int_{\mathrm{BV}}:O_{\mathrm{BV}}\to\mathbb{C},\qquad\text{such that}\qquad\int_{\mathrm{BV}}\Delta(-)=0.

In physics,
O_{\mathrm{BV}}
are functions on the space of zero modes at low energy.
\int_{\mathrm{BV}}
is a choice (related to the gauge fixing) of the integration map on zero modes. It will be
\hbar
-linearly extended when the quantum parameter
\hbar
is involved.

4.

A
\mathbf{k}
-linear map (encoding the path integral in physics)

\mathrm{\mathbf{Tr}}:C_{\bullet}(\mathrm{Obs})\rightarrow O_{\mathrm{BV},\mathbf{k}}=O_{\mathrm{BV}}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{C}((\hbar))

satisfying the
quantum master equation
(QME)

(d+\hbar\Delta)\mathrm{\mathbf{Tr}}=0.

In other words, QME says
\mathbf{Tr}
is a chain map intertwining
d
and
-\hbar\Delta
. In physics, it describes the quantum gauge consistency condition in terms of BV formalism. Index is obtained as the partition function of the model, which can be formulated as

\text{Index}=\int_{\mathrm{BV}}\mathrm{\mathbf{Tr}}(1).

In Section
2
, we review the theory of effective BV quantization. In Section
3
, we explain the 1d example of topological quantum mechanics on the circle and show how the above structures lead to the algebraic index theorem. In this case

•

The factorization algebra is the Weyl algebra:
\operatorname{Obs}=\mathcal{W}_{2n}
.

•

The factorization complex is the Hochschild chain complex
\left(C_{\bullet}(\mathcal{W}_{2n}),b\right)
.

•

BV algebra on zero modes:
\left(\mathcal{A},\Delta\right)=\left(\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathbb{R}^{2n}),\mathcal{L}_{\omega^{-1}}\right).

•

Free correlation map

\displaystyle\left\langle-\right\rangle:C_{\bullet}(\mathcal{W}_{2n})\to\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})((\hbar)),\quad b\mapsto\hbar\mathcal{L}_{\omega^{-1}}.

•

\operatorname{Index}=\int_{BV}\left\langle 1\right\rangle=\left[e^{\omega_{\hbar}/\hbar}\widehat{A}\right]
.

In Section
4
, we explain the 2d chiral example and the elliptic chiral analogue of algebra index via
\beta\gamma-bc
system. In this case, the factorization complex is the chiral chain complex of the corresponding vertex operator algebra. The trace map arising from BV quantization on elliptic curves will be called the
elliptic trace map
.

The above two examples in Section
2
and
4
share a special property: they are both UV finite theories. A conjectured structure for BV quantization of general UV finite theory is presented in Section
2.4
.

1d TQM

2d Chiral QFT

Associative algebra

Vertex operator algebra

Hochschild homology

Chiral homology

BV QME:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(\hbar\Delta+b)\langle-\rangle_{1d}=0}

BV QME:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\hbar\Delta+d_{ch})\langle-\rangle_{2d}=0}

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{O}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{O}_{n}\rangle_{1d}=
integrals

on the
compactified

configuration spaces of
\displaystyle S^{1}

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{O}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{O}_{n}\rangle_{2d}=
regularized

integrals
of singular forms on
\displaystyle\Sigma^{n}

Algebraic Index

Elliptic Chiral Algebraic Index

2
Effective Theory of BV Quantization

In this section, we review Costello’s homotopic theory of effective BV quantization
[
13
]
. This is the basic framework that we will use to establish the connection between BV quantization and index theories of algebraic type. We follow the presentation in
[
37
]
.

2.1
(-1)-shifted Symplectic Structure

We first explain that classical field theories and their quantizations have a universal description in terms of (-1)-shifted symplectic structure. This is particularly convenient to quantize gauge theories in the BV framework.

We start with the finite dimensional toy model. Let
(V,Q,\omega)
be a finite dimensional dg (differential graded) symplectic space. Here

•

V is a finite dimensional graded vector space.

•

Q:V\longrightarrow V
differential,
\deg Q=1
and
Q^{2}=0
.

•

\omega:\wedge^{2}V\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
non-degenerate pairing of deg=
-1
, that is,

\omega(a,b)=0,\text{ unless }|a|+|b|=1.

•

\omega
is Q-compatible
Q(\omega)=0
, i.e.,

\omega(Q(a),b)+(-1)^{|a|}\omega(a,Q(b))=0.

The non-degeneracy of
\omega
leads to linear isomorphisms

\displaystyle\omega:V^{\vee}

\displaystyle\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}V[1]

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow\quad\wedge^{2}(V^{\vee})

\displaystyle\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\wedge^{2}(V[1])\simeq Sym^{2}(V)[2]

\displaystyle\omega

\displaystyle\longleftrightarrow K[2]

Here
K=\omega^{-1}\in\Sym^{2}(V)
is the Poisson Kernel and

\deg(K)=1,~~Q(K)=0.

We obtain a triple
(A,Q,\Delta)
as follows

•

A=\mathcal{O}(V):=\widehat{\Sym}(V^{\vee})
(formal power series on V)

•

Q:A\longrightarrow A
derivation induced dually from
Q:V\longrightarrow V

•

BV operator

\Delta=\Delta_{K}:A\longrightarrow A

by contracting with the Poisson Kernel
K

\displaystyle\Delta_{K}:\Sym^{m}(V)\longrightarrow\Sym^{m-2}(V).

Explicitly, for
\alpha_{i}\in V^{\vee}

\displaystyle\Delta_{K}(\alpha_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\alpha_{m})=\sum_{i<j}\pm\langle K,\alpha_{i}\otimes\alpha_{j}\rangle\alpha_{1}\otimes\cdots\widehat{\alpha_{i}}\otimes\cdots\widehat{\alpha_{i}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\alpha_{m}.

•

\Delta_{K}
induces a BV bracket on
A
by

\{a,b\}:=\Delta_{K}(ab)-(\Delta_{K}a)b-(-1)^{|a|}a\Delta_{k}b

Here
|a|
is the degree of
a
.

•

Since
K
is
Q-
closed, we have

[Q,\Delta_{K}]:=Q\Delta_{K}+\Delta_{K}Q=0

The triple
(A,Q,\Delta)
is exactly the data of a DGBV. Given such a DGBV, we can talk about

•

Classical master equation
:

\displaystyle QI_{0}+\frac{1}{2}\left\{I_{0},I_{0}\right\}=0\qquad\text{for}\quad I_{0}\in A,\ \deg(I_{0})=0.

Then the classical BRST operator
\delta=Q+\left\{I_{0},-\right\}
satisfies
\delta^{2}=0
.

•

Quantum master equation
:

\displaystyle QI+\hbar I+\frac{1}{2}\left\{I,I\right\}=0\Longleftrightarrow(Q+\hbar\Delta)e^{I/\hbar}=0,\quad\text{for}\quad I=I_{0}+\hbar I_{1}+...\in A[[\hbar]].

Then the quantum BRST operator
\delta^{\hbar}=Q+\hbar\Delta+\left\{I,-\right\}
satisfies

(\delta^{\hbar})^{2}=0.

Classical Field Theory

Now we discuss the QFT situation. For our purpose, we focus on theories where fields are sections of vector bundles. A classical field theory can be organized into
\infty-
dimensional (-1)-shifted dg symplectic space

({\mathcal{E}},Q,\omega)

•

{\mathcal{E}}=\Gamma(X,E^{\bullet})
the space of fields. Here
E^{\bullet}
is a graded vector bundle on
X
.

•

({\mathcal{E}},Q)
elliptic complex

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{...}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{E}}^{-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{E}}^{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{E}}^{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{...}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}

For example,
Q=\bar{\partial}
or
d
.

•

\omega
: local (-1)-symplectic pairing

\omega(\alpha,\beta)=\int_{X}\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle,~~\forall\alpha,\beta\in{\mathcal{E}}

and compatible with Q.

Example 2.1

(Chern-Simons Theory)
.

Let
X
be a
\dim=3
manifold, and
{\mathfrak{g}}
be a Lie algebra with trace pairing
Tr:{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
. The space of fields is

{\mathcal{E}}=\Omega^{\bullet}(X,{\mathfrak{g}})[1].

The degree shifting
[1]
gives the following intepretation.

\Omega^{0}(X,{\mathfrak{g}})

\Omega^{1}(X,{\mathfrak{g}})

\Omega^{2}(X,{\mathfrak{g}})

\Omega^{3}(X,{\mathfrak{g}})

deg

-1

0

1

2

c

A

A^{\vee}

c^{\vee}

ghost

field

anti-field

anti-ghost

Q=d
is the de Rham differential. The
(-1)
-symplectic pairing is

\omega(\alpha,\beta)=\int_{X}Tr(\alpha\wedge\beta),\quad\alpha,\beta\in{\mathcal{E}}

which pairs
0
-forms with
3
-forms and pairs
1
-forms with
2
-forms.

Example 2.2

(Scalar Field Theory in BV formalism)
.

The field complex
{\mathcal{E}}
is
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\infty}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\infty}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\deg=0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\deg=1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{\vee}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q=\Delta+m^{2}}

The
(-1)
-symplectic pairing is

\omega(\phi,\phi^{\vee})=\int_{M}\phi\phi^{\vee}.

UV Problem

Let us now perform the same construction of DGBV algebra following the toy model. We first need the notion of "functions"
\mathcal{O}(V)=\widehat{\Sym}(V^{\vee})
on
V
.

•

linear function
: we have to take a continuous linear dual and so

{\mathcal{E}}^{\vee}=\Hom_{X}({\mathcal{E}},\mathbb{R})

is given by distributions.

•

({\mathcal{E}}^{\vee})^{\otimes n}=\Hom_{X\times...\times X}({\mathcal{E}}^{\otimes n},\mathbb{R})
are distributions on
X^{n}
. Here

{\mathcal{E}}^{\otimes n}=\Gamma(X^{n},E^{\boxtimes n})

is the completed tensor product. Thus

\Sym^{m}({\mathcal{E}}^{\vee}):=({\mathcal{E}}^{\vee})^{\otimes m}/S_{m}

is well-defined by distributions on
X^{m}
. As a result, we can form

\mathcal{O}({\mathcal{E}})=\prod_{m\geq 0}\Sym^{m}({\mathcal{E}}^{\vee})

representing (formal) functions on
{\mathcal{E}}
.

•

Q:{\mathcal{E}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{E}}
induces duality
Q:{\mathcal{E}}^{\vee}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{E}}^{\vee}
on distributions, and gives rise to

Q:\mathcal{O}({\mathcal{E}})\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}({\mathcal{E}}).

•

BV operator
: Let

K=\omega^{-1}
be the Poisson kernel as above. Since

\omega=\int\langle-,-\rangle

is an integral, its inverse
K
is a
\delta
-function distribution supported on the diagonal of
X\times X
. Thus

K
is
NOT
a smooth element in
\Sym^{2}({\mathcal{E}})
, but a distributional section. As a result, the naive BV operator

\Delta_{K}:\Sym^{m}({\mathcal{E}}^{\vee})\to\Sym^{m-2}({\mathcal{E}}^{\vee})

is
ill-defined
since we can not pair two distributions. This is essentially the Ultra-Violet problem. Renormalization is needed in the quantum theory!

Before we move on to discuss the issue of renormalization, let us point out that the classical theory is actually well-behaved. Let
\mathcal{O}_{loc}({\mathcal{E}})\subset\mathcal{O}({\mathcal{E}})
denote the subspace of local functionals, i.e., those by integrals of lagrangian densities

\mathcal{O}_{loc}({\mathcal{E}})=\left\{\int_{X}\mathscr{L}(...)\right\}

Although the BV operator
\Delta_{K}
is ill-defined, the associated BV bracket
\left\{-,-\right\}
is actually well-defined on local functionals since
\delta
-function can be integrated.

\left\{-,-\right\}:\mathcal{O}_{loc}({\mathcal{E}})\otimes\mathcal{O}_{loc}({\mathcal{E}})\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{loc}({\mathcal{E}})

K\sim\delta
=
\int_{X}(-)
\int_{X}\mathscr{L}_{1}
\int_{X}\mathscr{L}_{2}

In other words,

•

CME makes sense for local functionals

•

QME needs renormalization

We refer to
[
13
]
for detailed discussions on this issue.

Example 2.3

(Chern-Simons theory)
.

{\mathcal{E}}=\Omega^{\bullet}(X,{\mathfrak{g}})[1]

\Omega^{0}(X,{\mathfrak{g}})

\Omega^{1}(X,{\mathfrak{g}})

\Omega^{2}(X,{\mathfrak{g}})

\Omega^{3}(X,{\mathfrak{g}})

deg

-1

0

1

2

c

A

A^{\vee}

c^{\vee}

ghost

field

anti-field

anti-ghost

Let
\mathcal{A}=C+A+A^{\vee}+C^{\vee}\in{\mathcal{E}}
denote the master field collecting all components. Then the BV Chern-Simons action is

CS[\mathcal{A}]=\int_{X}Tr(\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{A}\wedge d\mathcal{A}+\frac{1}{6}\mathcal{A}\wedge[\mathcal{A},\mathcal{A}]).

This takes the same form as ordinary Chern-Simons except that we have expanded
\mathcal{A}
to get terms containing different components. The first quadratic term is denoted by
S_{free}
, the free part. The second cubic term is denoted by
I
, the interaction part.

CS
satisfies the following classical master equation

\left\{CS,CS\right\}=0.

This follows from the general argument that classical gauge theory is organized into a solution of classical master equation. Let us separate the free part and interaction

CS=S_{free}+I.

It is easy to see that

\left\{S_{free},-\right\}=d\ (=Q)

which corresponds to the de Rham differential. Thus

\displaystyle\left\{CS,CS\right\}=0

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\quad\frac{1}{2}\left\{S_{free},S_{free}\right\}+\left\{S_{free},I\right\}+\frac{1}{2}\left\{I,I\right\}=0

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\quad QI+\frac{1}{2}\left\{I,I\right\}=0

This is precisely the form of classical master equation in our DGBV.

2.2
Effective Renormalization

Assume we have a classical field theory
({\mathcal{E}}=\Gamma(X,E^{\bullet}),Q,\omega)
with classical local functional
I_{0}
(interaction) satisfying CME

QI_{0}+\frac{1}{2}\left\{I_{0},I_{0}\right\}=0.

As we explained before, quantization asks for

I_{0}\longrightarrow I=I_{0}+\hbar I_{1}+\hbar I_{2}+...\in\mathcal{O}({\mathcal{E}})[[\hbar]]

satisfying QME

``QI_{0}+\frac{1}{2}\left\{I_{0},I_{0}\right\}+\hbar\Delta I=0".

\underline{\text{Problem:}}

\Delta I
is
\underline{\text{NOT}}
well-defined. In the following, we explain Costello’s homotopic renormalization theory to solve this problem.

Toy Model

To motivate the construction, let us look back again at the toy model where
(V,Q,\omega)
is finite dimensional (-1)-shifted dg symplectic space. The Poisson kernel

K_{0}\in\Sym^{2}(V)

has
\deg(K_{0})=1
and satisfies
Q(K_{0})=0
. This allows us to construction the BV operator
\Delta_{0}
by contracting with
K_{0}
and obtain the DGBV triple
(A,Q,\Delta_{0})
.

Let us now consider the change of
K_{0}
by chain homotopy. Let

P\in\Sym^{2}(V),\quad\deg(P)=0.

Define

K_{P}=K_{0}+Q(P)=K_{0}+(Q\otimes 1+1\otimes Q)P.

We again have

•

K_{P}\in\Sym^{2}(V),\deg(K_{P})=1

•

Q(K_{P})=0

Thus we can construct a new BV operator

\Delta_{P}=\text{contraction with }K_{P}

such that
(\mathcal{O}(V),Q,\Delta_{P})
forms a new DGBV.

To see the relation with the original DGBV, denote

\partial_{P}:\Sym^{m}(V^{\vee})\longrightarrow\Sym^{m-2}(V^{\vee})

where
\partial_{P}
is a 2nd order operator of contracting with
P\in\Sym^{2}(V)

Proposition 2.4
.

The following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}(V)[[\hbar]]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}(V)[[\hbar]]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}(V)[[\hbar]]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}(V)[[\hbar]]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e^{\hbar\partial_{P}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q+\hbar\Delta_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q+\hbar\Delta_{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e^{\hbar\partial_{P}}}

i.e.

(Q+\hbar\Delta_{P})e^{\hbar\partial_{P}}=e^{\hbar\partial_{P}}(Q+\hbar\Delta_{0}).

Proof:.

This follows from the chain homotopy relation
K_{P}=K_{0}+Q(P)
.
∎

Corollary 2.5
.

Assume
I\in\mathcal{O}(V)[[\hbar]]
satisfies QME

(Q+\hbar\Delta_{0})e^{I/\hbar}=0

in the DGBV
(\mathcal{O}(V),Q,\Delta_{0})
. Then
\tilde{I}\in\mathcal{O}(V)[[\hbar]]
satisfies QME

(Q+\hbar\Delta_{P})e^{\tilde{I}/\hbar}=0

in the DGBV
(\mathcal{O}(V),Q,\Delta_{P})
. Here
\tilde{I}
is related to I by

e^{\tilde{I}/\hbar}=e^{\hbar\partial_{P}}e^{I/\hbar}

The operator
e^{\hbar\partial_{P}}
plays the role of integration with respect to the Gaussian measure. The relation
e^{\tilde{I}/\hbar}=e^{\hbar\partial_{P}}e^{I/\hbar}
can be read via Wick’s Theorem as

\displaystyle\hbox to245.03pt{\vbox to76.58pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-28.125pt\lower-85.5083pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

Here
I
serves as vertices and
P
for the propagator. Thus, Feynman diagrams give the required chain homotopy between different DGBV’s.

Back to QFT

Now consider the QFT set-up
({\mathcal{E}}=\Gamma(X,E^{\bullet}),Q,\omega)
. The problem is that the Poisson kernel
K_{0}=\omega^{-1}
is a
\delta
-function distribution which leads to a singular BV operator. Nevertheless we know
K_{0}
is
Q
-closed

\displaystyle Q(K_{0})=0.

\underline{\text{Costello's approach}}
: Using elliptic regularity

H^{\bullet}(\text{Distribution},Q)=H^{\bullet}(\text{Smooth},Q).

Here “Distribution" or “Smooth" means distributional or smooth sections of relevant tensor bundles of
E^{\bullet}
. Thus we can replace
K_{0}
by a smooth object in its
Q
-cohomology class by

K_{0}=K_{r}+Q(P_{r}).

Here
K_{r}
is smooth while
P_{r}
(called parametrix) is singular. Define

\displaystyle\Delta_{r}:\text{BV operator associated with $K_{r}$}

Since
K_{r}\in\Sym^{2}({\mathbb{E}})
is now smooth, the operator

\Delta_{r}:\mathcal{O}({\mathcal{E}})\to\mathcal{O}({\mathcal{E}})\text{ is well-defined}.

Definition 2.6
.

The DGBV
(\mathcal{O}({\mathcal{E}}),Q,\Delta_{r})
will be called the
effective DGBV
with respect to the regularization
r
.

Let
r^{\prime}
be another regularization with parametrix
P_{r^{\prime}}

K_{0}=K_{r^{\prime}}+Q(P_{r^{\prime}}).

Then the two regularized Poisson kernels differ by a chain homotopy

K_{r^{\prime}}-K_{r}=Q(P^{r^{\prime}}_{r})

where
P^{r^{\prime}}_{r}\in\Sym^{2}({\mathcal{E}})
is smooth. Let

\partial_{P^{r^{\prime}}_{r}}:\mathcal{O}({\mathcal{E}})\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}({\mathcal{E}})

be the 2nd order operator of contracting with the smooth kernel
P^{r^{\prime}}_{r}
.

The same argument as in the toy model gives the chain homotopy

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\big(\mathcal{O}({\mathcal{E}})[[\hbar]],Q+\hbar\Delta_{r}\big)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\big(\mathcal{O}({\mathcal{E}})[[\hbar]],Q+\hbar\Delta_{r}^{\prime}}\big)}
Homotopy RG flow (HRG)
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exp\left(\hbar\partial_{P^{r^{\prime}}_{r}}\right)}

Definition 2.7

(Costello
[
13
]
)
.

An effective perturbative quantization of
I_{0}
(which satisfies CME) is a family

I[r]\in\mathcal{O}({\mathcal{E}})[[\hbar]]\quad

(whichi is at least cubic modulo
\hbar
) for each choice of regularization
r
satisfying

•

Effective QME

(Q+\hbar\Delta_{r})e^{I[r]/\hbar}=0.

•

Homotopy RG flow

e^{I[r^{\prime}]/\hbar}=e^{\hbar\partial_{P^{r^{\prime}}_{r}}}e^{I[r]/\hbar}

which is equivalent to the Feynman diagram expansion

•

I[r]
is asymptotic local when
r\longrightarrow 0
and has the classical limit

\lim_{r\rightarrow 0}I_{0}[r]=I_{0}

Here is a pictue to illustrate what is going on. The situation is very similar to how residue is defined in algebraic geometry: we need to perturb the singularity and define residue at the deformed configuration, and show that all local deformations give the same answers. Here we use all "nearby" regularizations to define the unrenormalized point.

In practice, here are steps for constructing perturbative quantization.

\raisebox{-.9pt}{1}⃝

Construct counter-term
I^{\varepsilon}\in\hbar\mathcal{O}_{loc}({\mathcal{E}})[[\hbar]]
such that

e^{I[r]^{Naive}/\hbar}:=\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}(e^{\hbar{P^{r}_{\varepsilon}}}e^{(I_{0}+I^{\varepsilon})/\hbar})\quad\text{exists}

Then this naive family

\left\{I[r]^{Naive}\right\}_{r}
satisfies HRG by construction.

\raisebox{-.9pt}{2}⃝

The choice of counter-terms is not unique. We need to further correct
I^{\varepsilon}
such that
e^{I[r]/\hbar}
satisfies QME.

\raisebox{-.9pt}{1}⃝
is always possible by the method of counter-term.
\raisebox{-.9pt}{2}⃝
is
\underline{\text{NOT}}
always possible: obstruction may exist which is called "
gauge anomaly
" in physics terminology. There is a deformation-obstruction theory, which shows that the gauge anomaly lies in

H^{1}(\mathcal{O}_{loc}({\mathcal{E}}),Q+\{I_{0},-\}).

2.3
Heat Kernel Regularization

There are many ways of regularizations. One method that connects to geometry is the heat kernel regularization. Typically, fixing a choice of metric, we have

•

the adjoint of the elliptic operator
Q:{\mathcal{E}}\to{\mathcal{E}}
, denoted as
Q^{\dagger}:{\mathcal{E}}\to{\mathcal{E}}
,

•

assume
\left[Q,Q^{\dagger}\right]=QQ^{\dagger}+Q^{\dagger}Q
is a generalized Laplacian
1
1

1

We use
[-,-]
for graded commutator in this paper.
.

Thus we can define a heat operator
e^{-L\left[Q,Q^{\dagger}\right]}
for
L>0
. Let
K_{L}\in\operatorname{Sym}^{2}({\mathcal{E}})
be the kernel of the heat operator by

\displaystyle\left(e^{-L\left[Q,Q^{\dagger}\right]}\alpha\right)\left(x\right)=\int dy\left\langle K_{L}(x,y),\alpha(y)\right\rangle\quad\text{for }\alpha\in{\mathcal{E}}.

Here
\left\langle-,-\right\rangle
is the pairing from
\omega
.
Note that

•

K_{0}=\lim\limits_{L\to 0}K_{L}
is the
\delta
-function distribution
\omega^{-1}
,

•

K_{L}\in\operatorname{Sym}^{2}({\mathcal{E}})
is smooth for
L>0
.

Let
P_{L}
be the kernel of the operator
\int_{0}^{L}dt\ Q^{\dagger}e^{-t\left[Q,Q^{\dagger}\right]}
. Explicitly, we have

\displaystyle P_{L}=\int_{0}^{L}dt\left(Q^{\dagger}\otimes 1\right)K_{t}.

The operator equation

\displaystyle\left[Q,\int_{0}^{L}dt\ Q^{\dagger}e^{-t\left[Q,Q^{\dagger}\right]}\right]=\int_{0}^{L}dt\left[Q,Q^{\dagger}\right]e^{-t\left[Q,Q^{\dagger}\right]}=1-e^{-L\left[Q,Q^{\dagger}\right]}

can be translated into the kernel equation:

\displaystyle K_{0}-K_{L}=(Q\otimes 1+1\otimes Q)P_{L}

or simply written as

\displaystyle K_{0}-K_{L}=Q\left(P_{L}\right).

We can use
K_{L}
to define the effective QME.

Similarly, for
0<\varepsilon<L
, the operator equation is

\displaystyle\left[Q,\int_{\varepsilon}^{L}dt\ Q^{\dagger}e^{-t\left[Q,Q^{\dagger}\right]}\right]=e^{-\varepsilon\left[Q,Q^{\dagger}\right]}-e^{-L\left[Q,Q^{\dagger}\right]}

or

\displaystyle K_{\varepsilon}-K_{L}=(Q\otimes 1+1\otimes Q)P_{\varepsilon}^{L},

where
P^{L}_{\varepsilon}=\int_{\varepsilon}^{L}dt\left(Q^{\dagger}\otimes 1\right)K_{t}
is called the
regularized propagator
.
Now we can use
P^{L}_{\varepsilon}
to connect the effective QME at
\varepsilon
with the effective QME at
L
via the HRG.

Remark 2.8
.

P_{0}^{\infty}=\int_{0}^{\infty}dt\left(Q^{\dagger}\otimes 1\right)K_{t}
is the
full propagator
. At
t=0
, one will encounter ultraviolet (UV) divergence since there exists a singularity for the full propagator. On a non-compact manifold, one will encounter infrared (IR) divergence at
t=\infty
.

Consider the case when
X
is compact. Let

\mathbf{H}=\left\{\left.\varphi\in{\mathcal{E}}\ \right|\ \left[Q,Q^{\dagger}\right]\varphi=0\right\}\ =\left\{\left.\varphi\in{\mathcal{E}}\ \right|\ Q\varphi=Q^{\dagger}\varphi=0\right\}\ \simeq H^{\bullet}\left({\mathcal{E}},Q\right).

\mathbf{H}
is called the space of harmonics (or the zero modes), which is a finite-dimensional space (by Hodge theory).
Then we have

\displaystyle\hbox to545.86pt{\vbox to52.64pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 272.92859pt\lower-26.31947pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-272.92859pt}{-20.15974pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}}}

\Delta_{\mathbf{H}}
associated with
\omega_{\mathbf{H}}^{-1}

\Delta_{\mathbf{H}}=\Delta_{\infty}
. The essential story of effective BV quantization is depicted in the following diagram,

and
I[\infty]
solves the QME for
\left(\mathscr{O}(\mathbf{H}),\Delta_{\mathbf{H}}\right)
at
L=\infty
. The limit
L\to\infty
is an interesting point where we will find some finite-dimensional geometric data.

2.4
UV Finite Theory

In the BV formalism, the classical master equation

QI_{0}+\frac{1}{2}\{I_{0},I_{0}\}=0

is quantized to the quantum master equation

\text{``}QI+\hbar\Delta I+\frac{1}{2}\{I,I\}=0".

As we explained as above, this naive quantum master equation is ill-defined for local
I\in\mathcal{O}_{loc}({\mathcal{E}})
, and we have to use regularization to formulate the renormalized quantum master equation

QI[r]+\hbar\Delta_{r}I[r]+\frac{1}{2}\{I,I\}_{r}=0.

If the effective action at regularization
r
can be found as

e^{I[r]/\hbar}=\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}e^{\hbar\partial_{P^{r}_{\varepsilon}}}e^{I/\hbar}

for
I\in\mathcal{O}_{loc}(\varepsilon)[[\hbar]]
, i.e., the
\varepsilon
-dependent counter-term is
NOT
needed, we say the theory is UV finite. That is, for all regularized Feynman diagrams
\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}
exist.
P_{\varepsilon}^{L}
I
I

In this way, we can consider the limit

I[r]\rightarrow I,\qquad r\rightarrow 0,

and the
r\,\rightarrow\,0
limit of the renormalized quantum master equation

QI+\frac{1}{2}\{I,I\}+\cdots=0

will have a local expression that deforms the CME.

Conjecture
.

For UV finite theory, we expect to describe effective QME at
r\to 0
limit by

l_{1}^{\hbar}I+\frac{1}{2}l_{2}^{\hbar}(I,I)+\frac{1}{3!}l_{3}^{\hbar}(I,I,I)+\cdots=0

where
\{l_{1}^{\hbar},l_{2}^{\hbar},\cdots\}
defines a family of
L_{\infty}
-algebra parametrized by
\hbar
. They can be viewed as traded from
\Delta
in terms of the renormalization procedure.

There are two main classes of UV finite theories.

\raisebox{-.9pt}{1}⃝

Topological theory (Chern-Simons type) where
{\mathcal{E}}
is of the form of de Rham complex. The UV finite property was established by Kontsevich
[
34
]
and Axelrod-Singer
[
3
]
using the compactified configuration space.

\raisebox{-.9pt}{2}⃝

Holomorphic theory where
{\mathcal{E}}
is of the form of Dolbeault complex. In this case, the Feynman graph integral can not be extended to the compactified configuration space. Fortunately, the UV finite property still holds in general.

–

\dim_{\mathbb{C}}=1
: the UV property for chiral deformations is known to physicists via the method of point-splitting regularization (see for example Douglas
[
20
]
and Dijkgraaf
[
19
]
). This method is essentially Cauchy principal value, and a homological theory for such regularization was systematically developed in Li-Zhou
[
39
]
. In the framework of effective BV quantization, the UV finite property was established in Li
[
36
,
37
]
.

–

\dim_{\mathbb{C}}>1
: the method in
[
37
]
has been generalized for one loop graphs in Costello-Li
[
15
]
and Williams
[
48
]
. At higher loops, Budzik-Gaiotto-Kulp-Wu-Yu
[
10
]
presented a strategy to prove UV finiteness for Laman graphs. In
[
46
]
, Wang proved the UV finite property for all graphs on all
\mathbb{C}^{n}
using a compactified Schwinger space. This is further generalized to Kähler manifolds in Wang-Yan
[
47
]
.

It is an extremely interesting question to figure out
\{l_{1}^{\hbar},l_{2}^{\hbar},\cdots\}
in these examples. In Section
3
and Section
4
, we explain the simplest example in each of these two categories (Conjecture holds there) to illustrate the underlying rich structures.

3
Topological Quantum Mechanics

In this section we consider the example of topological quantum mechanics and illustrate its connection with deformation quantization and algebraic index theorem.

3.1
Deformation Quantization

The method of deformation quantization was developed in the series of papers by Bayen, Flato, Fronsdal, Lichnerowicz, Sternheimer
[
5
]
. The space of the real-valued (or complex-valued) functions on a phase space admits
two algebraic structures: a structure of
associative algebra
given by the usual product of
functions and a structure of Lie algebra given by the
Poisson bracket
. The study of the
properties of the deformations (in a suitable sense) of these two structures gives a new
invariant approach for quantum mechanics.

\displaystyle\hbox to237.03pt{\vbox to62.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 118.51396pt\lower-30.96904pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

This is essentially the quantization method in quantum mechanics, in which a function
f
on the classical phase space is quantized to an operator
\widehat{f}
.

Definition 3.1
.

A
Poisson manifold
is a pair
\left(X,P\right)
, where
X
is a smooth manifold, and
P\in\Gamma(X,\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\bigwedge$}^{2}TX)
satisfying
\left\{P,P\right\}_{\text{SN}}=0
.

Here
\left\{-,-\right\}_{\text{SN}}
is the
Schouten-Nijenhuis bracket
.
P
is called the Poisson tensor/bi-vector. In local coordinates, we can write

\displaystyle P=\sum_{i,j}P^{ij}(x)\partial_{i}\wedge\partial_{j}.

It defines a
Poisson bracket

\left\{-,-\right\}_{P}
on
C^{\infty}(X)
:

\displaystyle\left\{f,g\right\}_{P}\coloneqq\sum_{i,j}P^{ij}\partial_{i}f\partial_{j}g,\quad\forall f,g\in C^{\infty}(X).

The Poisson condition
\left\{P,P\right\}_{\text{SN}}=0
implies
\left\{-,-\right\}_{P}
satisfies Jacobi identity. Hence
\left\{-,-\right\}_{P}
naturally defines the Poisson algebra
\left(C^{\infty}(X),\left\{-,-\right\}_{P}\right)
.

Example 3.2
.

Let
(X,\omega)
be a symplectic manifold, where
\omega=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j}\omega_{ij}dx^{i}\wedge dx^{j}
is the symplectic 2-form. Let

P=\omega^{-1}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j}\omega^{ij}\partial_{i}\wedge\partial_{j},

where
(\omega^{ij})
is the inverse of
(\omega_{ij})
. Then

d\omega=0\,\Leftrightarrow\,\left\{P,P\right\}_{\text{SN}}=0.

Hence
(X,\omega^{-1})
defines a Poisson manifold.

Definition 3.3
.

A
star-product
on a Poisson manifold
(X,P)
is a
\mathbb{R}[[\hbar]]
-bilinear map

\displaystyle C^{\infty}(X)[[\hbar]]\times C^{\infty}(X)[[\hbar]]

\displaystyle\to C^{\infty}(x)[[\hbar]]

\displaystyle f\times g\quad

\displaystyle\mapsto f\star g=\sum_{k\geq 0}\hbar^{k}c_{k}\left(f,g\right)

such that

(1)

\star
is associative:
\left(f\star g\right)\star h=f\star\left(g\star h\right)
,

(2)

f\star g=fg+\mathcal{O}(\hbar),\quad\forall f,g\in C^{\infty}(X)
,

(3)

\frac{1}{2}\left(f\star g-g\star f\right)=\hbar\left\{f,g\right\}+\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{2}),\quad\forall f,g\in C^{\infty}(X)
,

(4)

c_{k}:C^{\infty}(X)\times C^{\infty}(X)\to C^{\infty}(X)
is a bidifferential operator.

Then
\left(C^{\infty}(X)[[\hbar]],\star\right)
is called a
deformation quantization
of
\left(X,P\right)
.

The definition of deformation quantization is purely algebraic. The
existence
of deformation quantization is highly nontrivial. DeWilde-Lecomte first
[
17
]
obtained the general existence of deformation quantization on symplectic manifolds via cohomological method. Fedosov
[
21
]
presented another beautiful approach on symplectic manifolds via differential geometric method. In the general case, Kontsevich
[
35
]
gave the complete solution for arbitrary Poisson manifold. The parameter
\hbar
is formal in the above definition of deformation quantization (only formal power series of
\hbar
is concerned). There is also a notion of strict deformation quantization introduced by Rieffel
[
45
]
in terms of
C^{*}
-algebras where
\hbar
is not formal.

Example 3.4
.

Let
X=\mathbb{R}^{2n}
, with Poisson tensor

P=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j}P^{ij}\partial_{i}\wedge\partial_{j}

Here
P^{ij}
are constants. Given
f(x),g(x)
, define the (formal) Moyal product
\star
by

\displaystyle(f\star g)(x)=\left.\exp{\left(\frac{\hbar}{2}\sum_{i,j}P^{ij}\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{i}}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{j}}\right)}\right|_{y=z=x}f(y)g(z)

or pictorially,

\displaystyle\hbox to131.4pt{\vbox to88.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-90.08249pt\lower-118.125pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

Then
\star
defines a deformation quantization.

Remark 3.5
.

If
P^{ij}\neq
constant, then the above formula does not work. How to correct this to a star product is the celebrated Formality Theorem in
[
35
]
.

Definition 3.6
.

Let
\left(V,\omega\right)
be a linear symplectic space, where
V\simeq\mathbb{R}^{2n}
and
\omega:\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\bigwedge$}^{2}V\to\mathbb{R}
is a symplectic pairing.
P=\omega^{-1}
is the Poison tensor. Write

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathscr{O}}(V)\coloneqq\widehat{\operatorname{Sym}}(V^{\vee})=\prod_{k\geq 0}\operatorname{Sym}^{k}(V^{\vee}),

where
V^{\vee}=\operatorname{Hom}(V,\mathbb{R})
is the linear dual of
V
. Then the Moyal product defines an associative algebra
\left(\widehat{\mathscr{O}}(V)[[\hbar]],\star\right)
, called the
(formal) Weyl algebra
.

3.2
Fedosov’s Geometric Method

We will focus on
symplectic manifolds
in the rest of this section. Fedosov
[
21
]
gave a simple and geometric construction of deformation quantization as follows.
On a symplectic manifold
(X,\omega)
, the tangent plane
T_{p}X
at each point
p\in X

is a linear symplectic space. Quantum fluctuations deform the algebra of functions on
T_{p}X
to the
associated
Weyl algebra
. These pointwise Weyl algebras form a vector bundle — the
Weyl bundle

\mathcal{W}(X)
on
X
.

Definition 3.7
.

Let
(X,\omega)
be a symplectic manifold.
We define the
Weyl bundle

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}(X)\coloneqq\prod_{k\geq 0}\operatorname{Sym}^{k}\left(T^{\ast}X\right)[[\hbar]].

So at each point
p\in X
, its fiber is

\displaystyle\left.\mathcal{W}(X)\right|_{p}=\widehat{\mathscr{O}}\left(T_{p}X\right)[[\hbar]].

Here
\widehat{\mathscr{O}}
refers to formal power series functions.

A local section of
\mathcal{W}(X)
is

\displaystyle\sigma(x,y)=\sum_{k,l\geq 0}\hbar^{k}a_{k,i_{1}\cdots i_{l}}(x)y^{i_{1}}\cdots y^{i_{l}},

where
\left\{x^{i}\right\}
are the base coordinates and
\left\{y^{i}\right\}
are the fiber coordinates;
a_{k,i_{1}\cdots i_{l}}(x)
are smooth functions.
Since
\left(T_{p}X,\left.\omega\right|_{T_{p}X}\right)
is linear symplectic, we have a fiberwise Moyal product, still denoted by
\star
. Thus
\left(\mathcal{W}(X),\star\right)
defines the
\infty
-dimensional bundle of algebras.

Let
\nabla
be a connection on
TX
which is torsion-free and compatible with
\omega
(i.e.
\nabla\omega=0
). Such connection is called a
symplectic connection
(which always exists and is not unique).
\nabla
induces a connection on all tensors. In particular, it defines a connection on
\mathcal{W}(X)
, still denoted by
\nabla
. Its curvature is

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}\sigma=\frac{1}{\hbar}\left[R_{\nabla},\sigma\right]_{\star},\quad\forall\sigma\in\Gamma(X,\mathcal{W}(X))

where

R_{\nabla}=\frac{1}{4}R_{ijkl}y^{i}y^{j}dx^{k}\wedge dx^{l}\in\Omega^{2}(X,\mathcal{W}(X))

is a 2-form valued in the Weyl bundle
\mathcal{W}(X)
;
R_{ijkl}=\omega_{im}R^{m}{}_{jkl}
is a curvature form contracted with the symplectic pairing.

Given a sequence of closed 2-forms
\left\{\omega_{k}\right\}_{k\geq 1}
on
X
, Fedosov showed that there exists a unique (up to gauge) connection on
\mathcal{W}(X)
of the form

\nabla+\frac{1}{\hbar}\left[\gamma,-\right]_{\star}

where
\gamma\in\Omega^{1}\left(X,\mathcal{W}(X)\right)
is a
\mathcal{W}(X)
-valued 1-form, satisfying certain initial conditions and the equation

\displaystyle\nabla\gamma+\frac{1}{2\hbar}\left[\gamma,\gamma\right]_{\star}+R_{\nabla}=\omega_{\hbar}\qquad\text{(Fedosov equation)}

where
\omega_{\hbar}=-\omega+\sum_{k\geq 1}\hbar^{k}\omega_{k}
.
Let

\displaystyle D=\nabla+\frac{1}{\hbar}\left[\gamma,-\right]_{\star}

be the Fedosov connection. Then the Fedosov equation implies

D^{2}=\frac{1}{\hbar}\left[\omega_{\hbar},-\right]_{\star}=0

since
\omega_{\hbar}
is constant along each fiber, thus a central term.
So we obtain a flat connection
D
on
\mathcal{W}(X)
.
Fedosov equation has the geometric
interpretation of BV quantum master equation
[
29
,
31
]
.

Let
\mathcal{W}_{D}(X)\coloneqq\left\{\left.\sigma\in\Gamma(X,\mathcal{W}(X))\ \right|D\sigma=0\right\}
be the space of flat sections. Then
\left(\mathcal{W}_{D}(X),\star\right)
is an associative algebra.
Let

\displaystyle\sigma:\mathcal{W}_{D}(X)\to C^{\infty}(X)[[\hbar]]

be the symbol map by sending
y\mapsto 0
. Then
\sigma
is an isomorphism, and

\displaystyle f\star g\mapsto\sigma\left(\sigma^{-1}\left(f\right)\star\sigma^{-1}\left(g\right)\right)

defines a deformation quantization.
\omega_{\hbar}
is the corresponding characteristic class (or moduli).

3.3
Algebraic Index Theorem

Given a deformation quantization
\left(C^{\infty}(X)[[\hbar]],\star\right)
on a symplectic manifold with characteristic class
\omega_{\hbar}
, there exists a unique
trace map

\displaystyle\Tr:C^{\infty}(X)[[\hbar]]\to\mathbb{R}((\hbar))

satisfying a normalization condition and the trace property:

\displaystyle\Tr\left(f\star g\right)=\Tr\left(g\star f\right).

Then the index is obtained as the partition function of the theory, which can be formulated as

\displaystyle\operatorname{Index}=\Tr(1)=\int_{X}e^{\omega_{\hbar}/\hbar}\widehat{A}(X),

where
\widehat{A}(X)
is the
(formal)
\widehat{A}
-genus
of
X
.
This is the simplest version of
algebraic index theorem
formulated by Fedosov
[
22
]
and Nest-Tsygan
[
42
]
as the algebraic analogue of Atiyah-Singer index theorem.

In the case of a vector bundle
E
on
X
, one can similarly construct a
deformation quantization
for
C^{\infty}\left(X,\operatorname{End}(E)\right)[[\hbar]]
and construct the trace map. In this case

\Tr(1)=\int_{X}e^{\omega_{\hbar}/\hbar}\operatorname{Ch}(E)\widehat{A}(X),

where
\operatorname{Ch}(E)
is the Chern character of the vector bundle
E
over
X
.

Relation with QFT

In supersymmetric (SUSY) QFT,
localization
often appears, in which the path integral on
{\mathcal{E}}
is often localized effectively to an equivalent integral on a finite-dimensional space
M\subset{\mathcal{E}}
describing some interesting moduli space:

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{E}}e^{iS/\hbar}=\int_{M}\left(-\right).

In topological QM, we find

\displaystyle\int_{\operatorname{Map}(S^{1},X)}e^{-S/\hbar}\overset{\hbar\to 0}{=}\int_{X}\left(-\right),

where
\operatorname{Map}(S^{1},X)
is a loop space, and
\int_{X}
indicates the localization to a
constant map
.
The path integral will be captured exactly by an effective theory in the formal neighborhood of
constant maps inside the full mapping space. Exact semi-classical approximation in
\hbar\to 0
allows
us to reduce the path integral into a meaningful integral on the moduli space of constant maps, i.e.,
X
.
The left-hand side usually gives a physics presentation of the analytic index of certain elliptic operator; the
right-hand side will end up with integrals of various curvature forms representing the topological index.

S^{1}
localized effective theory
X
S^{1}
\simeq\mathbb{R}^{2n}

Geometrically, a loop space is mapped to a localized neighborhood of a point in
X
(specified by the constant map), where a localized effective theory exists.
Locally, by the choice of Darboux coordinates,
X
can be thought of as a standard phase space,
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
.
The loop spaces are then glued together on
X
as a family of effective field theory. This can be done rigorously within the framework of effective BV quantization
[
31
]
: we find the following dictionary

•

Effective action
\leadsto\gamma
,

•

Quantum master equation
\leadsto
Fedosov equation,

•

BV integral
\leadsto
trace map,

•

Partition function
\leadsto
algebraic index.

3.4
Local Theory

In this section we study topological quantum mechanics in terms of the effective renormalization method, and explain how to use it to prove the algebraic index theorem. We follow the presentation in
[
29
,
31
]
.

Local model

Let us consider the standard phase space
(V,\omega)
, where
V\simeq\mathbb{R}^{2n}
with coordinates

(x^{1},\cdots,x^{n},x^{n+1},\cdots,x^{2n})=(q^{1},\cdots,q^{n},p_{1},\cdots,p_{n})

and

\omega=\sum_{i=1}^{n}dp_{i}\wedge dq^{i}.

Let
S^{1}_{dR}
be the (locally ringed) space with underlying topology of the circle
S^{1}
and the structure sheaf
\mathscr{O}(S^{1}_{dR})=\Omega^{\bullet}_{S^{1}}
, which is a differential graded ring of differential forms with the de Rham differential operator
d
. Consider the
local model
describing the space of maps

\displaystyle\varphi:S^{1}_{dR}\to V\simeq\mathbb{R}^{2n}.

Such a
\varphi
can be identified with an element in
\Omega^{\bullet}_{S^{1}}\otimes V
. Explicitly, let
\theta
be the coordinates on
S^{1}
(with the identification
\theta\sim\theta+1
). The space of maps can then be written as

\displaystyle\left\{\varphi\right\}=\left\{\mathbb{P}_{i}(\theta),\mathbb{Q}^{i}(\theta)\right\}_{i=1,\cdots,n},\quad\mathbb{P}_{i},\mathbb{Q}^{i}\in\Omega^{\bullet}_{S^{1}}.

Writing in form component,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{i}(\theta)=p_{i}(\theta)+\eta_{i}(\theta)d\theta,\quad\mathbb{Q}^{i}(\theta)=q^{i}(\theta)+\xi^{i}(\theta)d\theta.

So the space of fields is

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}=\Omega^{\bullet}_{S^{1}}\otimes V.

The triple
\left(\Omega^{\bullet}_{S^{1}}\otimes V,d,\int_{S^{1}}\left\langle-,-\right\rangle_{\omega}\right)
is an
\infty
-dimensional
(-1)
-dg symplectic space. The topological action is the free one:

\displaystyle S\left[\varphi\right]

\displaystyle\coloneqq\int_{S^{1}}\left\langle\varphi,d\varphi\right\rangle_{\omega}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\int_{S^{1}}\mathbb{P}_{i}d\mathbb{Q}^{i}=\sum_{i}\int_{S^{1}}p_{i}(\theta)dq^{i}(\theta).

Remark 3.8
.

This is the first-order formalism of topological quantum mechanics along the line of the
AKSZ
construction
[
1
]
.

Propagator

Let us choose the standard flat metric on
S^{1}
. Let
d^{\ast}
be the adjoint of
d
. The Laplacian is

\displaystyle dd^{\ast}+d^{\ast}d=-\left(\frac{d}{d\theta}\right)^{2}.

Let

\displaystyle\Pi=\omega^{-1}=\sum_{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{i}}\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial q^{i}}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{i}}\otimes\frac{\partial}{\partial q^{i}}-\frac{\partial}{\partial q^{i}}\otimes\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{i}}\right)\ \in\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\bigwedge$}^{2}V

be the Poisson bivector (or Poisson kernel).
Let

\displaystyle h_{t}\left(\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\right)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{4\pi t}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{-\frac{\left(\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}+n\right)^{2}}{4t}}

be the standard heat kernel on
S^{1}
. Then the regularized propagator is

\displaystyle P^{L}_{\varepsilon}=\int^{L}_{\varepsilon}\partial_{\theta_{1}}h_{t}\left(\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\right)dt\otimes\Pi\ \in{\mathcal{E}}\otimes{\mathcal{E}},

where
\int^{L}_{\varepsilon}\partial_{\theta_{1}}h_{t}\left(\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\right)dt\in C^{\infty}(S^{1}\times S^{1})
and
\Pi\in V\otimes V
. Let us denote

\displaystyle P^{S^{1}}\left(\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\right)=\int^{\infty}_{0}\partial_{\theta_{1}}h_{t}\left(\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\right)dt.

Then the full propagator is given by

\displaystyle P^{\infty}_{0}=P^{S^{1}}\otimes\Pi.

Proposition 3.9
.

P^{S^{1}}\left(\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\right)
is the following periodic function of
\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}\in\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}

\displaystyle P^{S^{1}}\left(\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\right)=\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}-\frac{1}{2}\quad\text{if }\quad 0<\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}<1.

P^{S^{1}}
is NOT a smooth function on
S^{1}\times S^{1}
(as expected), but it is
bounded
.

Correlation map

Let us denote the formal Weyl algebra

\displaystyle{\mathcal{W}_{2n}=\left(\mathbb{R}[[p_{i},q^{i}]]((\hbar)),\star\right)}\ ,

and the formal Weyl subalgebra

\displaystyle{\mathcal{W}_{2n}^{+}=\left(\mathbb{R}[[p_{i},q^{i}]][[\hbar]],\star\right)}\ ,

where
\star
is the Moyal product. We can identify the formal Weyl subalgebra as (formal) functions on
V
(via deformation quantization):

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{2n}^{+}\simeq\left(\widehat{\mathscr{O}}(V)[[\hbar]],\star\right).

Given
f_{0},f_{1},\cdots,f_{m}\in\mathcal{W}_{2n}
, we define

\mathcal{O}_{f_{0},f_{1},\cdots,f_{m}}\in\mathscr{O}({\mathcal{E}})((\hbar))

by

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{f_{0},f_{1},\cdots,f_{m}}\left[\varphi\right]\coloneqq\int_{0<\theta_{1}<\theta_{2}<\cdots<\theta_{m}<1}d\theta_{1}d\theta_{2}\cdots d\theta_{m}f^{(0)}_{0}\left(\varphi(\theta_{0})\right)f^{(1)}_{1}\left(\varphi(\theta_{1})\right)\cdots f^{(1)}_{m}\left(\varphi(\theta_{m})\right).

Here
\varphi\in\Omega^{\bullet}_{S^{1}}\otimes V
,
f(\varphi(\theta))=f(\mathbb{P}_{i}(\theta),\mathbb{Q}^{i}(\theta))\in\Omega^{\bullet}_{S^{1}}
,
and we decompose it as

f(\varphi(\theta))=f^{(0)}(\varphi(\theta))+f^{(1)}(\varphi(\theta))d\theta.

f_{0}^{(0)}
f_{m}^{(1)}d\theta_{m}
\cdots
f_{1}^{(1)}d\theta_{1}
f_{2}^{(1)}d\theta_{2}
\int_{\theta_{0}=0<\theta_{1}<\theta_{2}<\cdots<\theta_{m}<1}

Remark 3.10
.

f^{(1)}(\varphi)
is the topological descent of
f^{(0)}(\varphi)
in topological field theory.

Now let us apply the HRG flow

\exp{(\hbar P^{\infty}_{0})}\left(\mathcal{O}_{f_{0},f_{1},\cdots,f_{m}}\right).

Since
P^{\infty}_{0}
is bounded, it is convergent and well-defined! This is the
UV finite property
. As we have discussed, at
L=\infty
, we can view it as defining a function on zero modes

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}=H^{\bullet}\left(\Omega^{\bullet}_{S^{1}}\otimes V,d\right)=H^{\bullet}(S^{1})\otimes V=V\oplus Vd\theta.

On zero modes we have
\widehat{\mathscr{O}}(\mathbb{H})=\widehat{\Omega}^{-\bullet}_{2n}
forms on
V
.

Definition 3.11
.

We define the following correlation map:

\displaystyle\left\langle\cdots\right\rangle_{free}:\mathcal{W}_{2n}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{W}_{2n}\to\widehat{\Omega}^{-\bullet}_{2n}((\hbar))

by

\left\langle f_{0}\otimes f_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes f_{m}\right\rangle_{free}\coloneqq\left.\exp{(\hbar P^{\infty}_{0})}\left(\mathcal{O}_{f_{0},f_{1},\cdots,f_{m}}\right)\right|_{\mathbb{H}}.

In the path integral perspective, this is

\displaystyle\left\langle f_{0}\otimes f_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes f_{m}\right\rangle_{free}(\alpha)=\int_{\Im d^{\ast}\subset{\mathcal{E}}}\left[D\varphi\right]e^{-S[\varphi+\alpha]/\hbar}\mathcal{O}_{f_{0},f_{1},\cdots,f_{m}}[\varphi+\alpha],\quad\alpha\in\mathbb{H}=H^{\bullet}(S^{1})\otimes V.

Here the zero mode
\alpha
is viewed as the background field. It can also be represented as a Feynman diagram as follows.

P_{0}^{\infty}
P_{0}^{\infty}
P_{0}^{\infty}
P_{0}^{\infty}
\alpha
\alpha
\alpha
\alpha
\alpha
\alpha
\alpha
\alpha
\alpha
\cdots
\cdots
0
\cdots

Remark 3.12
.

See
[
38
]
for a probabilistic approach where the topological correlations above are constructed in terms of a large variance limit.

(Cyclic) Hochschild complex reviewed

Let
A
be a unital associative algebra and
\mskip 0.90001mu\overline{\mskip-0.90001mu{A}\mskip-0.90001mu}\mskip 0.90001mu\coloneqq A/(\mathbb{C}\cdot 1)
. Let
C_{-p}(A)\coloneqq A\otimes\mskip 0.90001mu\overline{\mskip-0.90001mu{A}\mskip-0.90001mu}\mskip 0.90001mu^{\otimes p}
be the cyclic
p
-chains. It carries a natural Hochschild differential

\displaystyle b:C_{-p}(A)\to C_{-p+1}(A),\quad p\geq 1

by

b(a_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{p})=(-1)^{p}a_{p}a_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{p-1}+\sum_{i=0}^{p-1}(-1)^{i}a_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{i}a_{i+1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{p}.

Then the associativity implies

b\circ b=0
. Thus,

(C_{-\bullet}(A),b)
defines the Hochschild chain complex. We can also define the Connes operator:

\displaystyle B:C_{-p}(A)\to C_{-p-1}(A)

by

B(a_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{p})=1\otimes a_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{p}+\sum_{i=1}^{p}(-1)^{pi}1\otimes a_{i}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{p}\otimes a_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{i-1}.

We have the following relations:

\displaystyle b^{2}=0,\quad B^{2}=0,\quad[b,B]=bB+Bb=0.

Let
u
be a formal variable of
\operatorname{deg}=2
. Then
(b+uB)^{2}=0
.
This defines a complex

\displaystyle CC^{per}_{-\bullet}(A)=\left(C_{-\bullet}(A)[u,u^{-1}],b+uB\right),

called the periodic cyclic complex. For a systematic reference, see Loday
[
41
]
.

Back to Correlation map

It is not hard to see that

\displaystyle\left\langle\cdots\right\rangle_{free}:C_{-p}\left(\mathcal{W}_{2n}\right)\to\widehat{\Omega}^{-p}_{2n}((\hbar)),

i.e.
\left\langle f_{0}\otimes f_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes f_{p}\right\rangle_{free}
is a
p
-form. Recall that
\widehat{\Omega}^{-\bullet}_{2n}
is equipped with a BV operator
\Delta=\mathcal{L}_{\omega^{-1}}=\mathcal{L}_{\Pi}
, the Lie derivative with respect to the Poisson bi-vector.

Proposition 3.13

(
[
31
]
)
.

\displaystyle\left\langle b(-)\right\rangle_{free}=\hbar\Delta\left\langle\cdots\right\rangle_{free},

\displaystyle\left\langle B(-)\right\rangle_{free}=d_{2n}\left\langle\cdots\right\rangle_{free}.

Here
d_{2n}:\widehat{\Omega}^{-\bullet}_{2n}\to\widehat{\Omega}^{-(\bullet+1)}_{2n}
is the de Rham differential.

In other words, the correlation map:

\displaystyle\left\langle\cdots\right\rangle_{free}:C_{-\bullet}(\mathcal{W}_{2n})\to\widehat{\Omega}^{-\bullet}_{2n}((\hbar))

intertwines
b
with
\hbar\Delta
and
B
with
d_{2n}
. We can combine the above two to get

\displaystyle\left\langle\cdots\right\rangle_{free}:CC^{per}_{-\bullet}(\mathcal{W}_{2n})\to\widehat{\Omega}^{-\bullet}_{2n}((\hbar))[u,u^{-1}]

which intertwines
b+uB
with
\hbar\Delta+ud_{2n}
.

BV integral on zero modes

We can define a
BV integration
map on the BV algebra
\left(\widehat{\Omega}^{-\bullet}_{2n},\Delta\right)

which is only non-zero on top forms
\widehat{\Omega}^{-2n}_{2n}
and sends

\displaystyle\beta\in\widehat{\Omega}^{-2n}_{2n}\mapsto\left.\frac{\hbar^{n}}{n!}\iota^{n}_{\Pi}\beta\right|_{p=q=0}.

This is the Berezin integral
[
7
]
over the purely fermionic super Lagrangian. We can extend this BV integration to an
S^{1}
-equivariant version by

\displaystyle\int_{BV}:\widehat{\Omega}^{-\bullet}_{2n}[u,u^{-1}]\to\mathbb{R}((\hbar))[u,u^{-1}],\quad\beta\mapsto\left.\left(u^{n}e^{\hbar\iota_{\Pi}/u}\beta\right)\right|_{p=q=0}.

Then it has the following property

\displaystyle\int_{BV}\left(\hbar\Delta+ud_{2n}\right)(-)=0.

Remark 3.14
.

For
\beta\in\widehat{\Omega}^{-\bullet}_{2n}
, the non-equivariant limit

\displaystyle\lim_{u\to 0}\int_{BV}\beta=\left.\frac{\hbar^{n}}{n!}\iota_{\Pi}^{n}\beta\right|_{p=q=0}

gives back the Berezin integral.

Combining the above maps, we define

\displaystyle\Tr\coloneqq\int_{BV}\circ\left\langle\cdots\right\rangle_{free}:CC^{per}_{-\bullet}(\mathcal{W}_{2n})\to\mathbb{R}((\hbar))[u,u^{-1}]

which satisfies the following equation:

\displaystyle\Tr\left((b+uB)(-)\right)=0.

Therefore
\Tr
descends to periodic cyclic homology. This essentially leads to the trace formula in Feigin-Felder-Shoikhet
[
23
]
.

Quantum Master Equation

We can generalize slightly by considering a graded vector space
V
with a
\operatorname{deg}=0
symplectic pairing
\omega
. We still have the canonical quantization
\left(\widehat{\mathscr{O}}(V)[[\hbar]],\star\right)
by Moayl product and similarly can define the BV algebra of forms

\left(\widehat{\Omega}^{-\bullet}_{V},\Delta=\mathcal{L}_{\omega^{-1}}\right).

The same trace map gives

\displaystyle\left\langle\cdots\right\rangle_{free}:C_{-\bullet}\left(\widehat{\mathscr{O}}(V)[[\hbar]]\right)\to\widehat{\Omega}^{-\bullet}_{V}((\hbar)),\quad b\mapsto\hbar\Delta.

Given
\gamma\in\widehat{\mathscr{O}}(V)[[\hbar]]
,
\operatorname{deg}(\gamma)=1
, it defines an action functional:

\displaystyle I_{\gamma}=\int_{S^{1}}\gamma(\varphi)\quad\forall\varphi\in\Omega^{\bullet}(S^{1})\otimes V.

Let us treat
I_{\gamma}
as an interaction and consider

\displaystyle\underbrace{\frac{1}{2}\int_{S^{1}}\left\langle\varphi,d\varphi\right\rangle}_{\text{free part}}+\underbrace{\int_{S^{1}}\gamma(\varphi)}_{I_{\gamma}}.

Then we run the HRG flow to get

\displaystyle e^{\frac{1}{\hbar}I_{\gamma}[\infty]}\coloneqq e^{\hbar\partial_{P^{\infty}_{0}}}e^{\frac{1}{\hbar}I_{\gamma}}

which is well-defined since
P^{\infty}_{0}
is bounded.

Let us now analyze the QME. By construction,

\displaystyle e^{\frac{1}{\hbar}I_{\gamma}[\infty]}=\left\langle 1\otimes e^{\gamma/\hbar}\right\rangle_{free}.

Assume
\gamma\star\gamma=\frac{1}{2}\left[\gamma,\gamma\right]_{\star}=0
. Then

\displaystyle\hbar\Delta e^{\frac{1}{\hbar}I_{\gamma}[\infty]}=\left\langle b\left(1\otimes e^{\gamma/\hbar}\right)\right\rangle_{free}=0.

Proposition 3.15

(
[
29
]
)
.

If
\left[\gamma,\gamma\right]_{\star}=0
, then the local interaction
I_{\gamma}=\int_{S^{1}}\gamma(\varphi)
defines a family of solutions of effective QME
I_{\gamma}[L]
at scale
L>0
by

\displaystyle e^{\frac{1}{\hbar}I_{\gamma}[L]}\coloneqq\lim_{\varepsilon\to\infty}e^{\hbar\partial_{P^{L}_{\varepsilon}}}e^{\frac{1}{\hbar}I_{\gamma}}.

3.5
Global Theory

Recall in Section
3.4
, we have discussed the first-order formalism of TQM such that in a local model with maps
\varphi:\Omega^{\bullet}_{S^{1}}\to V\simeq\mathbb{R}^{2n}
, the correlation map

\displaystyle\left\langle\cdots\right\rangle_{free}:C_{-\bullet}(\mathcal{W}_{2n})\to\widehat{\Omega}^{-\bullet}_{2n}((\hbar))

intertwines
b
with
\hbar\Delta
and
B
with
d_{2n}
.

In this section, we are going to glue this construction to a symplectic manifold and establish the algebraic index to universal Lie algebra
cohomology computations. The basic idea is to
glue
the local model
\Sigma\to T^{Model}\subset X
. In the following discussion, we borrow the presentation in
[
31
]
, where extensive references are given for related material.

Gluing via Gelfand-Kazhdan formal geometry

Definition 3.16
.

A
Harish-Chandra pair
is a pair
(\mathfrak{g},K)
, where
\mathfrak{g}
is a Lie algebra,
K
is a Lie group, with

•

an action of
K
on
\mathfrak{g}
:
K\xrightarrow{\ \rho\ }\operatorname{Aut}(\mathfrak{g})
,

•

a natural embedding:
\operatorname{Lie}(K)\xhookrightarrow{\ i\ }\mathfrak{g}
, where
\operatorname{Lie}(K)
is the Lie algebra associated with
K
,

such that they are compatible:

\displaystyle\hbox to120.68pt{\vbox to53.12pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 60.34023pt\lower-26.31947pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

.

A
(\mathfrak{g},K)
-module
is a vector space
V
with

•

K
on
V

\mathfrak{g}\to\operatorname{End}(V)
,

Definition 3.18
.

A
flat
(\mathfrak{g},K)
-bundle
over X is

•

a principal
K
-bundle
P\xrightarrow{\ \pi\ }X
,

•

a

-equivariant
\mathfrak{g}
-valued 1-form
\gamma\in\Omega^{1}(P,\mathfrak{g})
on

,

satisfying the following conditions:

(1)

\forall a\in\operatorname{Lie}(K)
, let

generated by
a
. Then we have the contraction
\gamma(\xi_{a})=a
such that

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

(2)

\gamma
satisfies the Maurer-Cartan equation

\displaystyle d\gamma+\frac{1}{2}\left[\gamma,\gamma\right]=0,

where
d
is the de Rham differential on
P
, and
\left[-,-\right]
is the Lie bracket in
\mathfrak{g}
.

Given a flat
(\mathfrak{g},K)
-bundle
P\to X
and
(\mathfrak{g},K)
-module
V
, let

\displaystyle\Omega^{\bullet}(P,V)\coloneqq\Omega^{\bullet}(P)\otimes V

denote differential forms on
P
valued in
V
.
It carries a connection

\displaystyle\nabla^{\gamma}=d+\gamma:\Omega^{\bullet}(P,V)\to\Omega^{\bullet+1}(P,V)

which is
flat
by the Maurer-Cartan equation.
The group
K
acts on
\Omega^{\bullet}(P)
and
V
, and hence inducing a natural action on
\Omega^{\bullet}(P,V)
. Let

\displaystyle V_{P}\coloneqq P\times_{K}V

be the vector bundle on
X
associated to the
K
-representation
V
. Let

\Omega^{\bullet}(X;V_{P})

be differential forms
on
X
valued in the bundle
V_{P}\to X
.
Similar to the usual principal bundle case,
\nabla^{\gamma}
induces a flat connection on
V_{P}\to X
. This defines a (de Rham) chain complex
\left(\Omega^{\bullet}(X;V_{P}),\nabla^{\gamma}\right)
, and
H^{\bullet}(X;V_{P})
denotes the corresponding de Rham cohomology.

We can descend Lie algebra cohomologies to geometric objects on
X
.

Definition 3.19
.

Let
V
be a
(\mathfrak{g},K)
-module. Define the
(\mathfrak{g},K)
relative Lie algebra cochain complex

\left(C^{\bullet}_{\text{Lie}}(\mathfrak{g},K;V),\partial_{Lie}\right)

by

\displaystyle C^{p}_{\text{Lie}}(\mathfrak{g},K;V)=\operatorname{Hom}_{K}\left(\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\bigwedge$}^{p}\left(\mathfrak{g}/\operatorname{Lie}(K)\right),V\right).

Here
\operatorname{Hom}_{K}
means
K
-equivariant linear maps.
\partial_{\text{Lie}}
is the Chevalley-Eilenberg differential if we view

C^{p}_{\text{Lie}}(\mathfrak{g},K;V)
as a subspace of the Lie algebra cochain
C^{p}_{\text{Lie}}(\mathfrak{g};V)
. Explicitly,
for
\alpha\in C^{p}_{\text{Lie}}(\mathfrak{g},K;V)
,

\displaystyle\left(\partial_{\text{Lie}}\alpha\right)\left(a_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge a_{p+1}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{p+1}(-1)^{i-1}a_{i}\cdot\alpha\left(a_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\widehat{a}_{i}\wedge\cdots\wedge a_{p+1}\right)

\displaystyle+\sum_{i<j}(-1)^{i+j}\alpha\left(\left[a_{i},a_{j}\right]\wedge\cdots\wedge\widehat{a}_{i}\wedge\cdots\wedge\widehat{a}_{j}\wedge\cdots\wedge a_{p+1}\right).

The corresponding cohomology is
H^{\bullet}_{\text{Lie}}(\mathfrak{g},K;V)
.

Given a
(\mathfrak{g},K)
-module
V
and flat
(\mathfrak{g},K)
-bundle
P\to X
with the flat connection
\gamma\in\Omega^{1}(P,\mathfrak{g})
.
We can define the
descent map
from the
(\mathfrak{g},K)
relative Lie algebra cochain complex to

V
-valued de Rham complex on
P
by

\displaystyle\operatorname{desc}:\left(C^{\bullet}_{Lie}(\mathfrak{g},K;V),\partial_{Lie}\right)\to\left(\Omega^{\bullet}(X;V_{P}),\nabla^{\gamma}\right),\quad\alpha\mapsto\alpha(\gamma,\cdots,\gamma)

inducing the cohomology descent map

\displaystyle\operatorname{desc}:H^{\bullet}_{Lie}(\mathfrak{g},K;V)\to H^{\bullet}(X;V_{P}).

Fedosov connection revisited

Recall the (formal) Weyl algebras

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{2n}=\mathbb{R}[[p_{i},q^{i}]]((\hbar)),\quad\mathcal{W}_{2n}^{+}=\mathbb{R}[[p_{i},q^{i}]][[\hbar]]

with the induced Lie algebra structure such that the Lie bracket is defined by

\displaystyle\left[f,g\right]\coloneqq\frac{1}{\hbar}\left[f,g\right]_{\star}=\frac{1}{\hbar}\left(f\star g-g\star f\right).

Let
\operatorname{Sp}_{2n}
be the symplectic group of linear transformations preserving the Poisson bivector
\Pi
. It acts on Weyl algebras by inner automorphisms. We can identify the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{sp}_{2n}
of
\operatorname{Sp}_{2n}
with quadratic polynomials in
\mathbb{R}[p_{i},q^{i}]
, and
\mathfrak{sp}_{2n}
is a Lie subalgebra of
\mathcal{W}_{2n}^{+}
.
The action
\operatorname{Sp}_{2n}\curvearrowright\mathbb{R}^{2n}
induces the action
\operatorname{Sp}_{2n}\curvearrowright\mathcal{W}_{2n}^{+}
. Hence,
\left(\mathcal{W}_{2n}^{+},\operatorname{Sp}_{2n}\right)
and
\left(\mathcal{W}_{2n},\operatorname{Sp}_{2n}\right)
are Harish-Chandra pairs.

Let
(X,\omega)
be a symplectic manifold, and
F_{\operatorname{Sp}}(X)
be the symplectic frame bundle. We have the Weyl bundles

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{+}_{X}=F_{\operatorname{Sp}}(X)\times_{\operatorname{Sp}_{2n}}\mathcal{W}_{2n}^{+},\quad\mathcal{W}_{X}=F_{\operatorname{Sp}}(X)\times_{\operatorname{Sp}_{2n}}\mathcal{W}_{2n}.

Consider the Harish-Chandra pair

\displaystyle(\mskip 0.90001mu\overline{\mskip-0.90001mu{\mathfrak{g}}\mskip-0.90001mu}\mskip 0.90001mu,K)=(\mathfrak{g}/Z(\mathfrak{g}),\operatorname{Sp}_{2n}),

where
\mathfrak{g}=\mathcal{W}_{2n}^{+}
, and
Z(\mathfrak{g})=\mathbb{R}[[\hbar]]
is the center of
\mathfrak{g}
,
Z(\mathfrak{g})\cap\mathfrak{sp}_{2n}=0
.
Fedosov constructed a flat
(\mskip 0.90001mu\overline{\mskip-0.90001mu{\mathfrak{g}}\mskip-0.90001mu}\mskip 0.90001mu,K)
-bundle
F_{\operatorname{Sp}}(X)\to X

and
H^{0}(X;\mathcal{W}_{X}^{+})
gives a
deformation quantization
.
Choose the trivial
(\mskip 0.90001mu\overline{\mskip-0.90001mu{\mathfrak{g}}\mskip-0.90001mu}\mskip 0.90001mu,K)
-module
\mathbb{R}((\hbar))
. Then

\displaystyle\operatorname{desc}:C^{\bullet}_{\text{Lie}}\left(\mskip 0.90001mu\overline{\mskip-0.90001mu{\mathcal{W}^{+}_{2n}}\mskip-0.90001mu}\mskip 0.90001mu,\mathfrak{sp}_{2n};\mathbb{R}((\hbar))\right)\to\Omega^{\bullet}_{X}((\hbar)).

This is the
Gelfand-Fuks map
.
Here

C^{\bullet}_{\text{Lie}}\left(\mskip 0.90001mu\overline{\mskip-0.90001mu{\mathcal{W}^{+}_{2n}}\mskip-0.90001mu}\mskip 0.90001mu,\mathfrak{sp}_{2n};\mathbb{R}((\hbar))\right)\simeq C^{\bullet}_{Lie}\left(\mathcal{W}^{+}_{2n},\mathfrak{sp}_{2n}\oplus Z(\mathcal{W}^{+}_{2n});\mathbb{R}((\hbar))\right).

Characteristic classes

Let us review the Chern-Weil construction of characteristic classes in Lie algebra cohomology.
They will descend to the usual characteristic forms via the Gelfand-Fuks map.

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a Lie algebra, and
\mathfrak{h}\subset\mathfrak{g}
be its Lie subalgebra. Let the projection map

\displaystyle\operatorname{pr}:\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{h}

be the
\mathfrak{h}
-equivariant splitting of the embedding
\mathfrak{h}\subset\mathfrak{g}
. In general
\operatorname{pr}
is not a Lie algebra homomorphism from
\mathfrak{g}
to
\mathfrak{h}
. The
failure
of
\operatorname{pr}
being a Lie algebra homomorphism gives
R\in\operatorname{Hom}\left(\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\bigwedge$}^{2}\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h}\right)
by

\displaystyle R(\alpha,\beta)=\left[\operatorname{pr}(\alpha),\operatorname{pr}(\beta)\right]_{\mathfrak{h}}-\operatorname{pr}\left[\alpha,\beta\right]_{\mathfrak{g}},\quad\alpha,\beta\in\mathfrak{g}.

The
\mathfrak{h}
-equivariance of
\operatorname{pr}
implies that
R\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathfrak{h}}\left(\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\bigwedge$}^{2}(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}),\mathfrak{h}\right)
.
R
is called the
curvature form
.
Let
\operatorname{Sym}^{m}(\mathfrak{h}^{\vee})^{\mathfrak{h}}
be
\mathfrak{h}
-invariant polynomials on
\mathfrak{h}
of homogeneous degree
\operatorname{deg}=m
. Given
P\in\operatorname{Sym}^{m}(\mathfrak{h}^{\vee})^{\mathfrak{h}}
,
we can
associate a cochain

P(R)\in C^{2m}_{\text{Lie}}(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h};\mathbb{R})

by the composition

\displaystyle P(R):\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\bigwedge$}^{2m}\mathfrak{g}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\bigwedge$}^{m}R}\operatorname{Sym}^{m}(\mathfrak{h})\xrightarrow{P}\mathbb{R}.

It can be checked that
\partial_{\text{Lie}}P(R)=0
, defining a cohomology class

[P(R)]\in H^{2m}(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h};\mathbb{R})

which does
not depend on the choice of
\operatorname{pr}
. Therefore we have the analogue of Chern-Weil characteristic map

\displaystyle\chi:\operatorname{Sym}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{h}^{\vee})^{\mathfrak{h}}\to H^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h};\mathbb{R}),\quad P\mapsto\chi(P)\coloneqq\left[P(R)\right].

Now we apply the above construction to the case where

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}=\mathcal{W}^{+}_{2n},\quad\mathfrak{h}=\mathfrak{sp}_{2n}\oplus Z(\mathfrak{g}).

Any element
f
in
\mathfrak{g}=\mathcal{W}^{+}_{2n}
can be uniquely written as a polynomial

f=f(y^{i},\hbar)
, with coordinates
(y^{1},\cdots,y^{n},y^{n+1},\cdots,y^{2n})=(p_{1},\cdots,p_{n},q^{1},\cdots,q^{n})
.
Define the
\mathfrak{h}
-equivariant projections

\displaystyle\operatorname{pr}_{1}(f)

\displaystyle=\left.\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j}\partial_{i}\partial_{j}f\right|_{y=\hbar=0}y^{i}y^{j}\in\mathfrak{sp}_{2n},

\displaystyle\operatorname{pr}_{3}(f)

\displaystyle=\left.f\right|_{y=0}\in Z(\mathfrak{g}).

We obtain the corresponding curvature

\displaystyle R_{1}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left[\operatorname{pr}_{1}(-),\operatorname{pr}_{1}(-)\right]-\operatorname{pr}_{1}\left[-,-\right]\ \in\operatorname{Hom}(\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\bigwedge$}^{2}\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{sp}_{2n}),

\displaystyle R_{3}

\displaystyle\coloneqq-\operatorname{pr}_{3}\left[-,-\right]\ \in\operatorname{Hom}(\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\bigwedge$}^{2}\mathfrak{g},\mathbb{R}[[\hbar]]).

Remark 3.20
.

A more general case can be considered when we incorporate vector bundles, where
\mathfrak{g}=\mathcal{W}^{+}_{2n}+\hbar\left(\mathfrak{gl}\left(\mathcal{W}^{+}_{2n}\right)\right)
,
\mathfrak{h}=\mathfrak{sp}_{2n}\oplus\hbar\mathfrak{gl}\oplus Z(\mathfrak{g})
. There the extra projection
\operatorname{pr}_{2}
and its corresponding curvature
R_{2}
are defined as elements in
\hbar\mathfrak{gl}
and
\operatorname{Hom}\left(\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\bigwedge$}^{2},\mathfrak{gl}\right)
, respectively.
It is worthwhile to point out that all the
\operatorname{Hom}
’s here are only
\mathbb{R}
-linear map, but not

\mathbb{R}[[\hbar]]
-linear, although
\mathfrak{g}
is a
\mathbb{R}[[\hbar]]
-module.

We now define the
\widehat{A}
-genus

\displaystyle\widehat{A}(\mathfrak{sp}_{2n})\coloneqq\left[\operatorname{det}\left(\frac{R_{1}/2}{\sinh{(R_{1}/2)}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right]\in H^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h};\mathbb{R}).

Under the descent map
\operatorname{desc}:H^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h};\mathbb{R}((\hbar)))\to H^{\bullet}(X)((\hbar))
via the Fedosov connection, it can be shown that

\displaystyle\operatorname{desc}\left(\widehat{A}(\mathfrak{sp}_{2n})\right)

\displaystyle=\widehat{A}(X),

\displaystyle\operatorname{desc}\left(R_{3}\right)

\displaystyle=\omega_{\hbar}-\hbar\omega.

Universal trace map

Recall that using
\Omega^{\bullet}_{S^{1}}\to\mathbb{R}^{2n}
, we have obtained

\displaystyle\Tr=\int_{BV}\circ\left\langle-\right\rangle_{free}:CC^{per}_{-\bullet}(\mathcal{W}_{2n})\to\mathbb{K}\coloneqq\mathbb{R}((\hbar))[u,u^{-1}].

Let us write

\displaystyle\Tr\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{K}}\left(CC^{per}_{-\bullet}(\mathcal{W}_{2n}),\mathbb{K}\right).

This is a
\left({\mathcal{W}^{+}_{2n}},\operatorname{Sp}_{2n}\right)
-module. Via the flat
\left({\mathcal{W}^{+}_{2n}},\operatorname{Sp}_{2n}\right)
-bundle
F_{\operatorname{Sp}}(X)\to X
, we obtain the associated bundle

\displaystyle E^{per}\coloneqq F_{\operatorname{Sp}}(X)\times_{\operatorname{Sp}_{2n}}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{K}}\left(CC^{per}_{-\bullet}(\mathcal{W}_{2n}),\mathbb{K}\right)

with induced flat connection
\nabla^{\gamma}
.

Recall the Weyl bundle
\mathcal{W}(X)=F_{\operatorname{Sp}}(X)\times_{\operatorname{Sp}_{2n}}\mathcal{W}_{2n}
with flat connection
\nabla^{\gamma}
.
We would like to glue
\Tr
on
X
. Let us denote
\delta
for the differential on
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{K}}\left(CC^{per}_{-\bullet}(\mathcal{W}_{2n}),\mathbb{K}\right)
induced from
b+uB
. So

\displaystyle\delta\Tr=\Tr\left((b+uB)(-)\right)=0.

We can view
\Tr
as defining an element in

\displaystyle C^{0}_{Lie}\left(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h};\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{K}}\left(CC^{per}_{-\bullet}(\mathcal{W}_{2n}),\mathbb{K}\right)\right),

where we take

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}=\mathcal{W}^{+}_{2n}/Z(\mathcal{W}^{+}_{2n}),\quad\mathfrak{h}=\mathfrak{sp}_{2n}.

However,
\Tr
is NOT
\mathfrak{g}
-invariant, i.e.
\partial_{\text{Lie}}\Tr\neq 0
. In other words,
\Tr
is NOT a map of
\left(\mathfrak{g},\operatorname{Sp}_{2n}\right)
-module. So
\Tr
can not be glued directly.

It is observed that
\partial_{\text{Lie}}\Tr=\delta(-)
.
It turns out that we have a canonical way to lift
\Tr
to

\displaystyle\widehat{\Tr}\in C^{\bullet}_{Lie}\left(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h};\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{K}}\left(CC^{per}_{-\bullet}(\mathcal{W}_{2n}),\mathbb{K}\right)\right)

such that

\displaystyle\widehat{\Tr}=\Tr+\text{terms in }C^{>0}_{Lie}\left(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h};\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{K}}\left(CC^{per}_{-\bullet}(\mathcal{W}_{2n}),\mathbb{K}\right)\right)

and satisfying the coupled cocycle condition

\displaystyle\left(\partial_{Lie}+\delta\right)\widehat{\Tr}=0.

\widehat{\Tr}
is called the
universal trace map
. Let us insert
1\in\mathcal{W}_{2n}
, then
\widehat{\Tr}(1)
is
\partial_{Lie}
-closed, which defines the
universal index
,
\left[\widehat{\Tr}(1)\right]\in H^{\bullet}_{Lie}(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h};\mathbb{K})
.

Theorem 3.21

(Universal algebraic index theorem)
.

\displaystyle\left[\widehat{\Tr}(1)\right]=u^{n}e^{-R_{3}/(u\hbar)}\widehat{A}(\mathfrak{sp}_{2n})_{u},

where for
A=\sum_{p\text{ even}}A_{p}
,
A_{P}\in H^{p}(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h};\mathbb{K})
,

\displaystyle A_{u}=\sum_{p}u^{-p/2}A_{p}.

This theorem is developed in the works of Feigin-Tsygan
[
24
]
, Feigin-Felder-Shoikhet
[
23
]
, Bressler-Nest-Tsygan
[
9
]
, and many others. This can be naturally generalized to the bundle case
[
31
]
(as well as an explicit formula as a byproduct).

Now we apply the Gelfand-Fuks (descent) map on
\widehat{\Tr}
, such that

\displaystyle\hbox to346.57pt{\vbox to52.64pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 173.28452pt\lower-26.31947pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

Let
\mathcal{W}_{D}(X)
be the space of flat sections of
\mathcal{W}(X)

that gives a
deformation quantization
. Then

\displaystyle\operatorname{desc}(\widehat{\Tr}):CC^{per}_{-\bullet}(\mathcal{W}_{D}(X))\to\Omega^{\bullet}(X)((\hbar))[u,u^{-1}],\quad b+uB\mapsto d_{X}.

In particular, it defines a
trace map
in deformation quantization by

\displaystyle f\in\mathcal{W}_{D}(X)\mapsto\int_{X}\operatorname{desc}(\widehat{\Tr})(f)\in\mathbb{R}((\hbar)).

We can show that
\int_{X}\operatorname{desc}(\widehat{\Tr})(f)
does not depend on
u
. By the
universal algebraic index theorem
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{X}\operatorname{desc}(\widehat{\Tr})(1)=\int_{X}e^{-\omega_{\hbar}/\hbar}\widehat{A}(X).

This gives the algebraic index theorem.

Construction of universal trace map
\widehat{\Tr}

We have the following relations
[
31
]
.

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

Let
\Theta:\mathfrak{g}\to\mathcal{W}^{+}_{2n}/Z(\mathcal{W}^{+}_{2n})=\mathfrak{g}

be the canonical identity map.
For each
f\in\mathcal{W}^{+}_{2n}/Z(\mathcal{W}^{+}_{2n})
, we have defined the local functional on
{\mathcal{E}}=\Omega^{\bullet}(S^{1})\otimes\mathbb{R}^{2n}
by

\displaystyle I_{f}(\varphi)=\int_{S^{1}}f(\varphi),\quad\varphi\in{\mathcal{E}}.

Then
\Theta
gives a map

\displaystyle I_{\Theta}:\mathfrak{g}\to\mathcal{O}_{loc}({\mathcal{E}}),\quad f\mapsto I_{\Theta(f)}.

We can view this map as

\displaystyle I_{\Theta}\in C^{1}(\mathfrak{g},\mathcal{O}_{loc}({\mathcal{E}}))=\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{loc}({\mathcal{E}}).

This allows constructing

\widehat{\Tr}\in C^{\bullet}_{Lie}\left(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h};\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{K}}\left(CC^{per}_{-\bullet}(\mathcal{W}_{2n}),\mathbb{K}\right)\right)
explicitly
[
31
]
by

\displaystyle\widehat{\Tr}\left(f_{0}\otimes f_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes f_{m}\right)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\int_{BV}\exp{\left(\hbar P^{\infty}_{0}\right)}\left(\mathcal{O}_{f_{0},f_{1},\cdots,f_{m}}e^{\frac{1}{\hbar}I_{\Theta}}\right)\ \in C^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h};\mathbb{K}),\quad f_{i}\in\mathcal{W}_{2n}

\displaystyle``=\int_{BV}\int_{\Im d^{\ast}\subset{\mathcal{E}}}e^{-\frac{1}{2\hbar}\int_{S^{1}}\left\langle\varphi,d\varphi\right\rangle+\frac{1}{\hbar}I_{\Theta}}\mathcal{O}_{f_{0},f_{1},\cdots,f_{m}}\,^{\prime\prime}.

Computation of index

The Weyl algebra
\mathcal{W}_{2n}
can be viewed as a family of associative algebras parameterized by
\hbar
.
This leads to the Gauss-Manin-Getzler connection
[
27
]

\nabla_{\hbar\partial_{\hbar}}
on
CC^{per}_{-\bullet}(\mathcal{W}_{2n})
.
The calculation of index consists of the following steps
[
31
]
:

(1)

Feynman diagram computation
implies

\displaystyle\widehat{\Tr}(1)=u^{n}e^{-R_{3}/(u\hbar)}\left(\underbrace{\widehat{A}(\mathfrak{sp}_{2n})_{u}}_{1-\text{loop computation}}+\mathcal{O}(\hbar)\right).

(2)

Computation of
Gauss-Manin-Getzler connection
shows

\nabla_{\hbar\partial_{\hbar}}\left(e^{R_{3}/(u\hbar)}\widehat{\Tr}(1)\right)\ \text{is $\partial_{Lie}$-exact}.

(3)

Combining (1) and (2), we find

\displaystyle\left[\widehat{\Tr}(1)\right]=\left[u^{n}e^{-R_{3}/(u\hbar)}\widehat{A}(\mathfrak{sp}_{2n})_{u}\right]\ \in H^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h};\mathbb{K}).

4
Two-dimensional Chiral Theory

We have discussed the first-order formalism of topological QM, where the fields are differential forms
\Omega^{\bullet}(S^{1},V)
on
S^{1}
valued in the vector bundle
V
with the de Rham differential
d
. Here
d
being part of the BRST operator implies that “translation is homologically trivial.” This defines a topological theory.

We will now consider 2d chiral models where the fields are differential forms
\Omega^{0,\bullet}(\Sigma,h)
with the Dolbeault differential
\mskip 0.90001mu\overline{\mskip-0.90001mu{\partial}\mskip-0.90001mu}\mskip 0.90001mu
. The Dolbeault differential being part of the BRST operator implies that “anti-holomorphic translation is homologically trivial,” which in turn defines a chiral (or holomorphic) theory.

In topological QM, the theory is
UV finite
. The general consideration in Section
2.4
applies and we find that the renormalized QME is traded to Moyal commutator and Fedosov equation. We will see that 2d chiral theory is also
UV finite
and we have a similar geometric result for QME
[
37
]
.

4.1
Vertex Algebra

As illustrated by the picture below, in 1d topological theory we have an associative algebra defined by the fusion of two operators
a\cdot b
; in 2d chiral theory we have (chiral) vertex algebra defined by
A_{(n)}B
. The algebras are found when one operator approaches another either on a line (for 1d) or on a plane (for 2d).
