On a plane, the “product” (binary operation) depends on the location holomorphically, leading to infinitely many binary operations.

Definition 4.1
.

A
vertex algebra
is a collection of data:

•

(space of states) a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded superspace
\mathcal{V}=\mathcal{V}_{even}\oplus\mathcal{V}_{odd}
,

•

(vacuum) a vector
{\left|0\right>}\in\mathcal{V}_{even}
,

•

(translation operator) an even linear map
T:\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{V}
,

•

(state-field correspondence) an even linear operation (vertex operation)

\displaystyle Y(-,z):\mathcal{V}\to\operatorname{End}\mathcal{V}[[z,z^{-1}]],\quad A\mapsto Y(A,z)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}A_{(n)}z^{-n-1}

such that
Y(A,z)B\in\mathcal{V}((z))
for any
A,B\in\mathcal{V}
.

The data are required to satisfy the following axioms:

•

(vacuum axiom)
Y({\left|0\right>},z)=1_{\mathcal{V}}
, i.e. for any
A\in\mathcal{V}
,

\displaystyle Y(A,z){\left|0\right>}\in\mathcal{V}[[z]]\quad\text{and}\quad\lim_{z\to 0}Y(A,z){\left|0\right>}=A,

•

(translation axiom)
T{\left|0\right>}=0
, i.e. for any
A\in\mathcal{V}
,

\displaystyle\left[T,Y(A,z)\right]=\partial_{z}Y(A,z),

•

(locality axiom) all
\left\{Y(A,z)\right\}_{a\in\mathcal{V}}
are mutually local.

Roughly speaking, mutual locality implies for any
A,B\in\mathcal{V}
, we can expand as

\displaystyle Y(A,z)Y(B,w)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{Y(A_{(n)}\cdot B,w)}{(z-w)^{n+1}}.

This is called the
operator product expansion (OPE)
.
\left\{A_{(n)}\cdot B\right\}
from the expansion coefficient can be viewed as defining an infinite tower of products. For simplicity, we will write

\displaystyle A(z)\equiv Y(A,z)\quad\text{for }A\in\mathcal{V}.

Then the OPE can be written as

\displaystyle A(z)B(w)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{A_{(n)}\cdot B(w)}{(z-w)^{n+1}}.

We also write, whenever only the
singular
parts matter,

\displaystyle A(z)B(w)\sim\sum_{n\geq 0}\frac{A_{(n)}\cdot B(w)}{(z-w)^{n+1}}.

Given a vertex algebra, we can define its
modes Lie algebra

\displaystyle\oint\mathcal{V}\coloneqq\operatorname{Span}_{\mathbb{C}}\left\{\oint dz\ z^{k}A(z)=A_{(k)}\right\}_{A\in\mathcal{V},\ k\in\mathbb{Z}}.

The Lie bracket of contour integrals is determined by the OPE,

\displaystyle\left[\oint dz\ z^{m}A(z),\ \oint dw\ w^{n}B(w)\right]=\oint dw\ w^{n}\oint_{w}dz\ z^{m}\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{A_{(j)}\cdot B(w)}{(z-w)^{j+1}},

where only the singular part matters in the integration. The Lie bracket is represented diagrammatically as follows.

We refer to
[
32
,
25
]
for a systematic discussion on vertex algebras.

Example 4.2

(
\beta\gamma
-system)
.

The
\beta\gamma
-system is generated by two
bosonic
fields
\beta(z),\gamma(z)
with OPE

\displaystyle\beta(z)\gamma(w)\sim\frac{\hbar}{z-w}\sim-\gamma(z)\beta(w).

The vertex algebra
\mathcal{V}
is identified with the differential ring

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}=\,:\mathrel{\mathbb{C}[[\partial^{i}\beta,\partial^{i}\gamma]]}:\,[[\hbar]],

where
\,:\mathrel{\ }:\,
is the
normal ordering operator
. The general OPE is obtained via
Wick contractions
. For example,

\displaystyle\,:\mathrel{\beta(z)\gamma(z)}:\,\,:\mathrel{\beta(w)\gamma(w)}:

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\frac{\hbar}{z-w}\,:\mathrel{\gamma(z)\beta(w)}:\,-\frac{\hbar}{z-w}\,:\mathrel{\beta(z)\gamma(w)}:\,}_{\text{1 contraction}}-\underbrace{\left(\frac{\hbar}{z-w}\right)^{2}}_{\text{2 contractions}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{\hbar}{z-w}\frac{(z-w)^{k}}{k!}\,:\mathrel{\partial^{k}\gamma(w)\beta(w)-\partial^{k}\beta(w)\gamma(w)}:\,-\frac{\hbar^{2}}{(z-w)^{2}}.

Example 4.3

(
bc
-system)
.

The
bc
-system is generated by two
fermionic
fields
b(z),c(z)
with OPE

\displaystyle b(z)c(w)\sim\frac{\hbar}{z-w}\sim c(z)b(w).

The vertex algebra
\mathcal{V}
is identified with the differential ring

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}=\,:\mathrel{\mathbb{C}[[\partial^{i}b,\partial^{i}c]]}:\,[[\hbar]].

The general OPE is generated in the similar way as the
\beta\gamma
-system (but we need to take care of the signs).

More generally, we can define a general
\beta\gamma-bc
system by considering a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded space

\displaystyle h=h_{0}\oplus h_{1}

with an even symplectic pairing

\displaystyle\left\langle-,-\right\rangle:\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\bigwedge$}^{2}h\to\mathbb{C}.

Let
\left\{a_{i}\right\}
be a basis of
h
, then we can define a vertex algebra
\mathcal{V}_{h}
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{h}=\,:\mathrel{\mathbb{C}[[\partial^{k}a_{i}]]}:\,[[\hbar]].

The OPE is generated by

\displaystyle a_{i}(z)a_{j}(w)\sim\frac{\hbar}{z-w}\left\langle a_{i},a_{j}\right\rangle.

In particular,
h_{0}
represents the copies of
\beta\gamma
-system;
h_{1}
represents the copies of
bc
-system.

4.2
Chiral Deformation of
\beta\gamma-bc
Systems

We consider the following data:

•

an elliptic curve
E
(topologically a torus
T^{2}
) with linear coordinate
z
such that
z\sim z+1\sim z+\tau
,

•

a graded symplectic space
h=h_{0}\oplus h_{1}
with an even symplectic pairing
\left\langle-,-\right\rangle
.

This defines a field theory in BV formalism where the space of fields is

{\mathcal{E}}=\Omega^{0,\bullet}(E)\otimes h

with
(-1)
-symplectic pair by

\omega(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})=\int_{E}dz\left\langle\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}\right\rangle,\quad\varphi_{i}\in{\mathcal{E}}.

Note that
\omega
has
\operatorname{deg}=-1
since we need exactly one
\mskip 0.90001mu\overline{\mskip-0.90001mu{dz}\mskip-0.90001mu}\mskip 0.90001mu
from
\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}
to be integrated.

The free theory is given by

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{E}dz\left\langle\varphi,\mskip 0.90001mu\overline{\mskip-0.90001mu{\partial}\mskip-0.90001mu}\mskip 0.90001mu\varphi\right\rangle,\quad\varphi\in{\mathcal{E}}.

The local quantum observables form exactly
\beta\gamma-bc
system. The propagator is given by the Szegö kernel

\displaystyle\mskip 0.90001mu\overline{\mskip-0.90001mu{\partial}\mskip-0.90001mu}\mskip 0.90001mu^{-1}\sim\frac{1}{z-w}+\text{regular}.

We would like to consider a general interacting theory by turning on
chiral deformations
of the form

\displaystyle\int\mathcal{L}\left(\varphi,\partial_{z}\varphi,\partial_{z}^{2}\varphi,\cdots\right)

which involves only
holomorphic
derivatives. This is related to the vertex algebra

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{h^{\vee}}=\mathbb{C}[[\partial^{i}h^{\vee}]][[\hbar]]

as follows. Define a map

\displaystyle I:\mathcal{V}_{h^{\vee}}\to\mathscr{O}_{loc}({\mathcal{E}}),\quad\gamma\mapsto I_{\gamma}.

Explicitly, if
\gamma=\sum\partial^{k_{1}}a_{1}\cdots\partial^{k_{m}}a_{m}
, then

\displaystyle I_{\gamma}(\varphi)=i\int_{E}dz\sum\pm\partial^{k_{1}}_{z}a_{1}(\varphi)\cdots\partial^{k_{m}}_{z}a_{m}(\varphi).

Here
a_{i}\in h^{\vee}
and
a_{i}(\varphi)\in\omega^{0,\bullet}(E)
.

Theorem 4.4

(
[
37
]
)
.

For any
\gamma\in\mathcal{V}_{h^{\vee}}
, the chiral deformed theory

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{E}dz\left\langle\varphi,\mskip 0.90001mu\overline{\mskip-0.90001mu{\partial}\mskip-0.90001mu}\mskip 0.90001mu\varphi\right\rangle+I_{\gamma}(\varphi)

is
UV finite
in the sense that the limit

\displaystyle e^{\frac{1}{\hbar}I_{\gamma}[L]}=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}e^{\hbar\partial_{P^{L}_{\varepsilon}}}e^{\frac{1}{\hbar}I_{\gamma}}\quad\text{exists.}

Remark 4.5
.

The proof of the UV finiteness theorem is a bit technical. The reason is different from topological QM, where we saw that the propagator is bounded (although not continuous). Here the graph integral is NOT absolutely convergent. See Section
4.3
for another geometric interpretation
[
39
]
of this fact.

Once we have a well-defined
I_{\gamma}[L]
described above, we can formulate the effective QME

\displaystyle\mskip 0.90001mu\overline{\mskip-0.90001mu{\partial}\mskip-0.90001mu}\mskip 0.90001muI_{\gamma}[L]+\hbar\Delta_{L}I_{\gamma}[L]+\frac{1}{2}\left\{I_{\gamma}[L],I_{\gamma}[L]\right\}_{L}=0

and ask for the condition of
\gamma
to satisfy the equation. It turns out that the answer is very simple.

Theorem 4.6

(
[
37
]
)
.

Consider
\gamma\in\mathcal{V}_{h^{\vee}}
and the effective functional
I_{\gamma}[L]
defined above via the UV finiteness. Then
I_{\gamma}[L]
satisfies the effective QME

\displaystyle\mskip 0.90001mu\overline{\mskip-0.90001mu{\partial}\mskip-0.90001mu}\mskip 0.90001muI_{\gamma}[L]+\hbar\Delta_{L}I_{\gamma}[L]+\frac{1}{2}\left\{I_{\gamma}[L],I_{\gamma}[L]\right\}_{L}=0

if and only if

\displaystyle\left[\oint\gamma,\oint\gamma\right]=0\ \in\oint\mathcal{V}.

Remark 4.7
.

The local quantum observable of the chiral deformed theory is the vertex algebra
H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{V}_{h^{\vee}},[\oint\gamma,-])
. So
[\oint\gamma,-]
plays the role of BRST reduction. Reversing this reasoning, vertex algebras coming from the BRST reduction of free field realizations can be realized via the model of chiral deformations above.

The above theorem can be glued for a
chiral
\sigma
-model

\displaystyle\varphi:E\to X

which produces a bundle
\mathcal{V}(X)\to X
of chiral vertex algebras on
X
. Then the solution of effective QME asks for a flat connection on
\mathcal{V}(X)
of the form

\displaystyle D=d+\frac{1}{\hbar}\left[\oint\gamma,-\right],\ \text{such that}\ D^{2}=0.

Here
\gamma\in\Omega^{1}\left(X,\mathcal{V}(X)\right)
and
\oint\gamma
is fiberwise chiral mode operator. This can be viewed as the
chiral analogue of Fedosov connection
.

4.3
Regularized Integral and UV Finiteness

The propagator
\mskip 0.90001mu\overline{\mskip-0.90001mu{\partial}\mskip-0.90001mu}\mskip 0.90001mu^{-1}
is given by the Szegö kernel which exhibits holomorphic poles
\frac{1}{z-w}
along the diagonal. In general, the Feynman diagram involves
\int_{\Sigma^{n}}\Omega
, where
\Omega
exhibits holomorphic poles of arbitrary order when
z_{i}\to z_{j}
.
It turns out that such looking divergent integral has an intrinsic
regularization
via its conformal structure.

For simplicity, we start by considering such an integral
\int_{\Sigma}\omega
.
Here
\Sigma
is a Riemann surface, possibly with boundary
\partial\Sigma
,
\omega
is a 2-from on
\Sigma
with meromorphic poles of arbitrary order along a finite set
D\subset\Sigma
and
D\cap\partial\Sigma=\varnothing
.

{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@stroke@opacity{1}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-opacity} \lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill-opacity} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{90.51pt}{-24.6233pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

Let
p\in D
and
z
be a local coordinate centered at
p
. Then locally
\omega
can be written as

\displaystyle\omega=\frac{\eta}{z^{n}}

where
\eta
is smooth, and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Since the pole order can be arbitrarily large, the naive integral
\int_{\Sigma}\omega
is divergent in general. One homological way out of this divergence problem
[
39
]
is as follows.
We can decompose
\omega
into

\displaystyle\omega=\alpha+\partial\beta,

where
\alpha
is a 2-form with at most
logarithmic pole
along
D
,
\beta
is a
(0,1)
-form with
arbitrary order of poles
along
D
, and
\partial=dz\frac{\partial}{\partial z}
is the holomorphic de Rham differential. Such a decomposition
exists
and is
not unique
.

Definition 4.8

(
[
39
]
)
.

Define the
regularized integral

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Sigma}\omega\coloneqq\int_{\Sigma}\alpha+\int_{\partial\Sigma}\beta

as a recipe to integrate the singular form
\omega
on
\Sigma
. It has the following properties

•

it does not depend on the choice of
\alpha,\beta
, and is equivalent to the Cauchy principal value,

•

\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Sigma}
is invariant under conformal transformations,

•

\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Sigma}\partial(-)=\int_{\partial\Sigma}(-)
on
(0,1)
-form with meromorphic poles,

•

\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Sigma}\mskip 0.90001mu\overline{\mskip-0.90001mu{\partial}\mskip-0.90001mu}\mskip 0.90001mu(-)=\operatorname{Res}(-)
on
(1,0)
-form with meromorphic poles.

The regularized integral extends the usual integral for smooth forms, i.e., the following diagram is commutative:

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

Here
\mathcal{A}^{2}(\Sigma)
means smooth 2-forms on
\Sigma
, and
\mathcal{A}^{2}(\Sigma,\star D)
means smooth 2-forms on
\Sigma-D
with meromorphic poles of arbitrary order around
D
.

We can use this to define integrals on configuration space of
\Sigma

\displaystyle\operatorname{Conf}_{n}(\Sigma)=\Sigma^{n}-\Delta=\left\{\left.(p_{1},\cdots,p_{n})\in\Sigma^{n}\right|p_{i}\neq p_{j},\forall i\neq j\right\}

and define

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Sigma^{n}}:\mathcal{A}^{2n}(\Sigma^{n},\star\Delta)\to\mathbb{C}

by iterating

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Sigma^{n}}(-)=\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Sigma}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Sigma}\cdots\ \mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Sigma}(-).

It does NOT depend on the choice of the ordering of the factors in
\Sigma^{n}
: Fubini-type theorem holds.
This gives an intrinsically regularized meaning for
\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Sigma^{n}}\Omega
, where
\Omega
is the Feynman diagram integrand.
This explains why the theory is UV finite.

4.4
Chiral Homology and Elliptic Trace

Intuitively, chiral chain complex can be viewed as a 2d chiral analogue of Hochschild chain complex.

•

In
[
54
]
, Zhu studied the space of genus 1 conformal blocks (i.e. the 0th elliptic chiral homology).

•

In
[
6
]
, Beilinson and Drinfeld developed the chiral homology theory on general algebraic curves.

The construction of Beilinson-Drinfeld.

We briefly review the construction of Beilinson-Drinfeld and refer to
[
30
]
for further details related to the purpose of the current discussion. Let
S
denote the category of finite non-empty sets whose morphisms are surjections.
Given the following data:

•

a category of right
\mathcal{D}
-modules
\mathcal{M}(X)
on
X=\Sigma
,

•

a category of right
\mathcal{D}
-modules
\mathcal{M}(X^{S})
on
X^{S}
, such that each element
M\in\mathcal{M}(X^{S})
is a collection that assigns every finite index set
I\in S
a right
\mathcal{D}
-module
\mathcal{M}_{X^{I}}
on the product
X^{I}
satisfying certain compatibility conditions,

•

there is an exact fully faithful embedding

\displaystyle\Delta^{(S)}_{\star}:\mathcal{M}(X)\hookrightarrow\mathcal{M}(X^{S})

via the diagonal map
\Delta^{(I)}:X\hookrightarrow X^{I}
,

•

\mathcal{M}(X^{S})
carries a (chiral) tensor structure
\otimes^{\operatorname{ch}}
,

Then a chiral algebra
\mathcal{A}
is a
Lie algebraic object
via
\Delta^{(S)}_{\star}
.

Remark 4.9
.

The chiral algebra
\mathcal{A}
collects all “normal ordered operators” in physics terminology.

We consider the Chevalley-Eilenberg (CE) complex

\displaystyle\left(C(\mathcal{A}),d_{\operatorname{CE}}\right)=\left(\bigoplus_{\bullet>0}\operatorname{Sym}^{\bullet}_{\otimes^{\operatorname{ch}}}\left(\Delta^{(S)}_{\star}\mathcal{A}[1]\right),d_{\operatorname{CE}}\right).

The chiral homology for this complex is

\displaystyle C^{\operatorname{ch}}(X,\mathcal{A})=R\Gamma_{DR}(X^{S},C(\mathcal{A})).

We will focus on
\beta\gamma-bc
system, where the vertex operator algebra (VOA)
\mathcal{V}^{\beta\gamma-bc}
gives rise to a chiral algebra
\mathcal{A}^{\beta\gamma-bc}
. The following theorem gives the corresponding elliptic trace map in terms of renormalization group flow.

Theorem 4.10

(
[
30
]
)
.

Let
E
be an elliptic curve. Then the HRG flow gives a map

\displaystyle\left\langle-\right\rangle_{2d}:C^{\operatorname{ch}}(E,\mathcal{A}^{\beta\gamma-bc})\to\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{BV}}((\hbar))

satisfying the QME

(d_{\operatorname{ch}}+\hbar\Delta)\left\langle-\right\rangle_{2d}=0.

•

Here
\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{BV}}
is the space of functions on zero modes of the
\beta\gamma-bc
system, which carries a structure of BV algebra.
\Delta
is the corresponding BV operator.

•

\left\langle-\right\rangle_{2d}
is defined by

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathcal{O}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{O}_{n}\right\rangle_{2d}\coloneqq\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{E^{n}}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{d^{2}z_{i}}{\Im\tau}\right)\left\langle\mathcal{O}_{1}(z_{1})\cdots\mathcal{O}_{n}(z_{n})\right\rangle

where
\left\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{1}(z_{1})\cdots{\mathcal{O}}_{n}(z_{n})\right\rangle
is the correlation function computed via Feynman diagrammatics, and
\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int
is the regularized integral.

•

The QME says that
{\langle-\rangle_{2d}}
intertwines the chiral differential of the elliptic chiral chain complex with the BV operator
-\hbar\Delta
of the zero-mode algebra
\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{BV}}((\hbar)))
. Moreover,
{\langle-\rangle_{2d}}
is shown to be a quasi-isomorphism.

•

The BV trace with universal background leads to
Witten genus
.

Theorem
4.10
establishes the construction of BV quantization and trace map outlined in the introduction. The Witten genus can be viewed as describing an elliptic chiral analogue of the algebraic index. The computation of Witten genus in BV quantization follows essentially from similar arguments in Costello
[
12
]
and Gorbounov-Gwilliam-Williams
[
28
]
.

4.5
2d
\to
1d Reduction

We summarize our discussion as follows.

1d TQM

2d chiral QFT

Associative algebra

Vertex/chiral algebra

Hochschild homology

Chiral homology

QME
(\hbar\Delta+b)\left\langle-\right\rangle_{1d}=0

QME
(\hbar\Delta+d_{\operatorname{ch}})\left\langle-\right\rangle_{2d}=0

\left\langle\mathcal{O}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{O}_{n}\right\rangle_{1d}=\int_{\mskip 0.90001mu\overline{\mskip-0.90001mu{\operatorname{Conf}_{n}(S^{1})}\mskip-0.90001mu}\mskip 0.90001mu}

\left\langle\mathcal{O}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{O}_{n}\right\rangle_{2d}=\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Sigma^{n}}

In physics, the partition functions/correlation functions on elliptic curves are described by reducing to a quantum mechanical system on
S^{1}
.

Now we can define 2d chiral correlation function using
regularized integral

\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{E}
. In 1d, operators are described by
A
-cycle
\oint_{A}
. These two integrals are not exactly the same, but related to each other by
holomorphic limit
.

Theorem 4.11

(
[
39
]
)
.

Let
\Phi(z_{1},\cdots,z_{n};\tau)
be a meromorphic elliptic function on
\mathbb{C}^{n}\times\mathbb{H}
which is holomorphic away from diagonals. Let
A_{1},\cdots,A_{n}
be
n
disjoint
A
-cycles on the elliptic curve
E_{\tau}=\mathbb{C}/(\mathbb{Z}\oplus\tau\mathbb{Z})

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

Then the regularized integral

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{E^{n}_{\tau}}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{d^{2}z_{i}}{\Im\tau}\right)\Phi(z_{1},\cdots,z_{n};\tau)

lies in
\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{H}}\left[\frac{1}{\Im\tau}\right]
. Moreover, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{\mskip 0.90001mu\overline{\mskip-0.90001mu{\tau}\mskip-0.90001mu}\mskip 0.90001mu\to\infty}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{E^{n}_{\tau}}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{d^{2}z_{i}}{\Im\tau}\right)\Phi=\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\sigma\in S_{n}}\oint_{A_{\sigma(1)}}dz_{1}\cdots\oint_{A_{\sigma(n)}}dz_{n}\ \Phi,

where
S_{n}
is the
n
-th permutation group and

\lim_{\mskip 0.90001mu\overline{\mskip-0.90001mu{\tau}\mskip-0.90001mu}\mskip 0.90001mu\to\infty}:\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{H}}\left[\frac{1}{\Im\tau}\right]\to\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{H}}\quad\text{is the map sending}\quad\frac{1}{\Im\tau}\to 0.

This theorem gives a precise relation on reduction of torus to circle

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{E^{n}}\xrightarrow{\lim\limits_{\mskip 0.90001mu\overline{\mskip-0.90001mu{\tau}\mskip-0.90001mu}\mskip 0.90001mu\to\infty}}\text{averaged }\oint_{A}.

The anti-holomorphic dependence of
\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{E^{n}}
on the moduli
\tau
is actually fully described by the holomorphic anomaly equation
[
40
]
.

Furthermore, if
\Phi(z_{1},\cdots,z_{n};\tau)
is modular of weight
m
, then its regularized integral
\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{E^{n}_{\tau}}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{d^{2}z_{i}}{\Im\tau}\right)\Phi(z_{1},\cdots,z_{n};\tau)
is modular of weight
m
and thus an almost holomorphic modular forms
[
33
]
. The holomorphic limit by averaged
\oint_{A}
is a quasi-modular form of weight
m
.

We apply the above theorem to 2d chiral correlations on elliptic curves. This leads to the following relation between the elliptic trace map in Theorem
4.10
and Weyl-ordered operators by
A
-cycle integrals

\displaystyle\lim_{\mskip 0.90001mu\overline{\mskip-0.90001mu{\tau}\mskip-0.90001mu}\mskip 0.90001mu\to\infty}\left\langle\mathcal{O}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{O}_{n}\right\rangle_{2d}=

\displaystyle\lim_{\mskip 0.90001mu\overline{\mskip-0.90001mu{\tau}\mskip-0.90001mu}\mskip 0.90001mu\to\infty}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{E^{n}_{\tau}}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{d^{2}z_{i}}{\Im\tau}\right)\left\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{1}(z_{1})\cdots{\mathcal{O}}_{n}(z_{n})\right\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\sigma\in S_{n}}\oint_{A_{\sigma(1)}}dz_{1}\cdots\oint_{A_{\sigma(n)}}dz_{n}\ \left\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{1}(z_{1})\cdots{\mathcal{O}}_{n}(z_{n})\right\rangle.

This can be viewed as a reduction formula from 2d to 1d. This formula illustrates an interesting relationship between regularization and modularity/quasi-modularity.

4.6
Application to Mirror Symmetry

Mirror symmetry is about a duality between

{\small\text{\boxed{\text{symplectic geometry}} ({A-model})}\Longleftrightarrow\text{\boxed{\text{complex geometry}} ({B-model)}}}

Here is a cartoon to illustrate how such mirror relation arises from physics.

\textstyle{\int_{\Map(\Sigma_{g},X)}\left(\text{A-model}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
SUSY localize
Fourier transform
\textstyle{\int_{\Map(\Sigma_{g},X^{\prime})}\left(\text{B-model}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
SUSY localize
\textstyle{\int_{\text{Holomorphic maps}(\Sigma_{g},X)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\int_{\text{Constant maps}(\Sigma_{g},X^{\prime})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
Gromov-Witten Theory
Hodge theory/Kodaira-Spencer gravity

Consider the example of elliptic curves, whose mirrors are elliptic curves as well. The full quantum B-model (quantum BCOV theory as developed in
[
14
]
) on elliptic curves (including all gravitational descendents) is completely solved in
[
37
]
. The so-called stationary sector is described by the chiral deformation of chiral boson

S=\int\partial\phi\wedge\bar{\partial}\phi+\sum_{k\geq 0}\int\eta_{k}{W^{(k+2)}(\partial_{z}\phi)\over k+2}

where

W^{(k)}(\partial_{z}\phi)=(\partial_{z}\phi)^{k}+O(\hbar)

are the bosonic realization of the
W_{1+\infty}
-algebra. The holomorphic limit
\bar{\tau}\to\infty
(explained in Section
4.5
) of the generating function of
S
on the elliptic curve coincides with the Gromov-Witten invariants on the mirror computed by Dijkgraaf
[
18
]
and Okounkov-Pandharipande
[
44
]
. In this case, we find
[
37
]

\text{Quantum Mirror Symmetry=Boson-Fermion Correspondence}.
